<a id='question-122'></a>

### Question 122: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 22 (CHAPTER)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (specifically the alternative pathway) in innate immunity and its dysregulation in certain diseases, including the identification of key regulators like Factor H. (理解补体激活（特别是替代途径）在先天免疫中的作用及其在某些疾病中的失调，包括关键调节因子如Factor H的识别。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of smoking and hypertension presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe flank pain, hematuria, and fever. He is diagnosed with a urinary tract infection (UTI) complicated by pyelonephritis. Urine culture grows *Escherichia coli*. He is treated with intravenous antibiotics, but his condition worsens, developing sepsis and acute kidney injury. Laboratory investigations reveal markedly decreased serum C3 levels, normal C4 levels, and elevated levels of soluble C5b-9 (terminal complement complex, TCC). Further testing shows a deficiency in the plasma protein Factor H. Which of the following mechanisms best explains the patient's severe clinical presentation and laboratory findings?

(A) Impaired opsonization of *E. coli* by C3b, leading to reduced phagocytosis by neutrophils.
(B) Uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway on the surface of *E. coli* and host cells, leading to excessive inflammation and tissue damage.
(C) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to insufficient C5b-6-7-8-9 complex formation.
(D) Inability of *E. coli* to produce lipopolysaccharide (LPS), preventing activation of the classical complement pathway.
(E) Increased production of C1 inhibitor, leading to excessive inhibition of the classical and lectin complement pathways.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 55-year-old male, smoker, hypertensive, presents with UTI/pyelonephritis symptoms (flank pain, hematuria, fever). This suggests a bacterial infection, likely involving the urinary tract.
- **Microbiology:** *E. coli* isolated from urine culture. *E. coli* is a common cause of UTIs and can activate complement.
- **Clinical Course:** Worsening condition despite antibiotics, sepsis, acute kidney injury. This indicates a severe, uncontrolled inflammatory response and tissue damage.
- **Laboratory Findings:**
    - Decreased C3: Indicates consumption of C3, a central component of all complement pathways.
    - Normal C4: Suggests the classical and lectin pathways are not significantly activated, pointing towards predominant activation of the alternative pathway.
    - Elevated soluble C5b-9 (TCC): Indicates excessive activation of the terminal complement pathway, leading to membrane damage.
    - Deficiency in Factor H: Factor H is a key regulator of the alternative complement pathway, preventing its excessive activation on host surfaces. Its deficiency leads to uncontrolled C3 convertase formation and activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism explaining the severe presentation and lab findings, linking the Factor H deficiency to the clinical outcome.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization (C3b), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and direct lysis (MAC). There are three pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), lectin (mannose-binding lectin-dependent), and alternative (spontaneous activation).
- **Alternative Pathway:** This pathway is continuously active at a low level. C3 spontaneously hydrolyzes to C3(H2O), which binds Factor B. Factor D cleaves Factor B to form C3bBb (the alternative pathway C3 convertase). Factor I, regulated by Factor H, inactivates C3bBb. Factor H binds to C3b and acts as a cofactor for Factor I. Factor P (properdin) stabilizes C3bBb.
- **Factor H Deficiency:** Factor H is the primary regulator of the alternative pathway. Deficiency leads to uncontrolled formation and activity of the C3 convertase (C3bBb) on microbial surfaces and, critically, on host cell surfaces. This results in excessive C3 cleavage, consumption of C3 (low serum C3), generation of C3a and C5a (inflammation), and formation of the MAC (C5b-9), leading to tissue damage (e.g., kidney injury, thrombosis). The elevated soluble C5b-9 (sC5b-9) reflects the systemic activation of the terminal pathway.
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Factor H deficiency is associated with atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS), characterized by microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury, often triggered by infections like UTI. The uncontrolled complement activation damages endothelial cells, particularly in the kidneys.
- **Link to Vignette:** The patient's presentation (UTI, sepsis, AKI), lab findings (low C3, normal C4, high sC5b-9), and known Factor H deficiency perfectly fit the pathophysiology of aHUS triggered by *E. coli* infection due to dysregulated alternative complement activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Impaired opsonization by C3b would *reduce* phagocytosis and inflammation, contradicting the patient's severe sepsis and high sC5b-9. While C3 is consumed, the primary problem isn't lack of opsonization but *excessive* activation leading to damage.
- (C): Defective MAC formation would lead to increased susceptibility to certain infections (e.g., *Neisseria*), not the severe inflammation and tissue damage seen here. The patient has *elevated* sC5b-9, indicating MAC formation is occurring excessively, not defectively.
- (D): *E. coli* produces LPS, which is a potent activator of the alternative complement pathway. Impaired LPS production would *reduce* complement activation, contradicting the findings.
- (E): Increased C1 inhibitor would inhibit the classical and lectin pathways, leading to low C4 (which is normal here) and potentially reduced complement activation overall, again contradicting the findings of low C3 and high sC5b-9. C1 inhibitor deficiency causes uncontrolled classical pathway activation, leading to low C4 and C3.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Factor H Deficiency (Correct)                                  | C1 Inhibitor Deficiency (Distractor E)                               | C3 Deficiency (Related but different)                                  | Classical Pathway Defect (e.g., C2 def) | Alternative Pathway Defect (e.g., Factor D def) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Regulator of Alternative Pathway                             | Inhibitor of Classical & Lectin Pathways                             | Central Component (C3)                                                  | Component of Classical Pathway            | Component of Alternative Pathway               |
| **Complement Levels**   | Low C3, Normal C4, High sC5b-9                               | Low C4, Low C3, Normal/High sC5b-9                                  | Low C3, Low C4, Low sC5b-9                                              | Normal C3, Low C4, Normal sC5b-9          | Normal C3, Normal C4, Low sC5b-9              |
| **Pathway Affected**    | Primarily Alternative                                          | Primarily Classical & Lectin                                         | All Pathways (Central)                                                  | Classical                                 | Alternative                                    |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | aHUS, glomerulonephritis, often triggered by infection        | Hereditary Angioedema (HAE)                                         | Increased susceptibility to pyogenic infections (especially encapsulated) | Increased susceptibility to pyogenic infections | Increased susceptibility to pyogenic infections |
| **Key Regulator/Component** | Factor H                                                     | C1 Inhibitor                                                         | C3                                                                     | C2, C4                                    | Factor D, Factor B, Properdin                  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Factor H deficiency leads to uncontrolled alternative complement pathway activation, resulting in consumption of C3, increased terminal complement complex formation (sC5b-9), and tissue damage (e.g., aHUS). This contrasts with classical pathway defects (low C4, normal C3) or deficiencies in terminal components (normal C3/C4, low sC5b-9). Remember that low C3 with normal C4 strongly suggests alternative pathway overactivation.

<a id='question-123'></a>

### Question 123: Immunodeficiency and Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly those affecting T-cell function and cytokine signaling, and their association with susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. Specifically, it focuses on the role of STAT signaling in T-cell development and function and its link to susceptibility to *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* infection. (考察特定免疫缺陷，特别是影响T细胞功能和细胞因子信号传导的缺陷，以及它们与特定机会性感染的易感性之间的关系。具体关注STAT信号传导在T细胞发育和功能中的作用及其与易感于结核分枝杆菌感染的关系。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male presents to the clinic with a 3-month history of persistent cough, night sweats, weight loss, and low-grade fever. He reports no significant past medical history, no known allergies, and denies recent travel or exposure to individuals with active tuberculosis. He is not on any immunosuppressive medications. A chest X-ray reveals bilateral apical infiltrates with cavitation. Sputum samples are positive for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on Ziehl-Neelsen staining. Cultures confirm the presence of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*. Further investigation reveals that the patient has a history of recurrent severe viral infections since childhood, including persistent warts and multiple episodes of pneumonia requiring hospitalization. Laboratory tests show normal B-cell counts and immunoglobulin levels, but markedly reduced numbers of CD4+ T cells and CD8+ T cells. Flow cytometry analysis of his T cells reveals impaired phosphorylation of STAT1 and STAT3 upon stimulation with IL-2 and IL-15, respectively.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying defect in this patient?

(A) Defective MHC class II expression on antigen-presenting cells
(B) Mutation in the gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc)
(C) Deficiency in the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA)
(D) Mutation in the gene encoding Janus kinase 2 (JAK2)
(E) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ)

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 35-year-old male with symptoms suggestive of pulmonary tuberculosis (cough, night sweats, weight loss, fever, apical infiltrates, AFB+ sputum). This points towards an infection typically controlled by cell-mediated immunity.
- **Immunological History:** Recurrent severe viral infections (warts, pneumonia) since childhood suggests a primary immunodeficiency affecting T-cell function.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Normal B-cell counts and immunoglobulin levels rule out primary B-cell defects or combined immunodeficiencies affecting B-cell maturation. Markedly reduced CD4+ and CD8+ T cells indicate a T-cell deficiency.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Impaired phosphorylation of STAT1 (stimulated by IL-2) and STAT3 (stimulated by IL-15) is a crucial clue. STAT proteins are key transcription factors downstream of cytokine receptor signaling, particularly for IL-2, IL-7, IL-15, and common gamma chain cytokines. Impaired phosphorylation means the signaling pathway is defective.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The patient has a T-cell deficiency leading to susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* and recurrent viral infections. The specific defect identified is impaired STAT signaling downstream of IL-2 and IL-15 receptors. This pattern strongly suggests a defect in the JAK-STAT pathway itself.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **JAK-STAT Pathway:** Cytokines like IL-2, IL-7, IL-15 bind to their receptors, which associate with Janus kinases (JAKs). JAKs phosphorylate the receptor and STAT proteins. Phosphorylated STATs dimerize, translocate to the nucleus, and regulate gene transcription involved in cell growth, differentiation, and survival.
- **IL-2 and IL-15 Signaling:** IL-2 is crucial for T-cell proliferation and survival, primarily acting on CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. IL-15 is essential for the development, survival, and homeostasis of memory CD8+ T cells and NK cells. Both signal through JAK1/JAK3 and STAT5 (for IL-2) or JAK1/JAK2 and STAT5 (for IL-15). STAT1 is activated by IFN-γ signaling (JAK1/JAK2) and is important for antiviral responses. STAT3 is activated by IL-6 signaling (JAK1/JAK2) and is involved in Th17 differentiation. The vignette specifically mentions impaired STAT1 phosphorylation upon IL-2 stimulation (which primarily activates STAT5) and impaired STAT3 phosphorylation upon IL-15 stimulation (which primarily activates STAT5). While STAT5 is the primary target for IL-2 and IL-15, cross-talk and complex signaling can occur. However, the most direct link between impaired T-cell development/function and defective JAK-STAT signaling, particularly affecting IL-2 and IL-15 responses, points towards a JAK defect. Mutations in JAK2 are known to cause immunodeficiency characterized by reduced T-cell numbers and impaired cytokine signaling.
- **Tuberculosis Pathogenesis:** *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is an intracellular pathogen primarily controlled by cell-mediated immunity, specifically activated macrophages and Th1 responses involving IFN-γ produced by T cells. Defects in T-cell function, particularly CD4+ T cells, lead to increased susceptibility to TB.
- **STAT Signaling and T-cell Development:** Proper T-cell development in the thymus requires signaling through IL-7 (via JAK1/JAK3 and STAT5) and IL-15 (via JAK1/JAK2 and STAT5). Defects in these pathways can lead to lymphopenia.

##### 3. Linking the Defect to the Clinical Picture:
- A mutation in JAK2 would impair signaling downstream of IL-2 and IL-15 receptors, leading to reduced T-cell numbers (lymphopenia) and impaired T-cell function (proliferation, survival). This explains the patient's susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis* and recurrent viral infections. The flow cytometry finding of impaired STAT phosphorylation (specifically mentioning STAT1 and STAT3, although JAK2 primarily affects STAT5 signaling downstream of IL-2/IL-15) is consistent with a defect in the JAK-STAT pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Defective MHC class II expression on antigen-presenting cells: This would lead to Bare Lymphocyte Syndrome (BLS), characterized by a severe lack of CD4+ T cells due to failure of positive selection in the thymus. While patients are susceptible to infections, the primary defect is in antigen presentation, not cytokine signaling. Flow cytometry would show absent MHC class II, not impaired STAT phosphorylation.
- (B) Mutation in the gene encoding the common gamma chain (γc): The γc (encoded by *IL2RG*) is a component of receptors for IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, and IL-21. Mutations cause X-linked Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (X-SCID), characterized by profound T-cell and NK-cell lymphopenia, but B-cell numbers are usually normal. While STAT5 signaling is affected, the vignette describes impaired STAT1 and STAT3 phosphorylation, and the clinical picture (late presentation, specific STAT findings) is less typical for classic X-SCID. Also, JAK2 is more directly implicated in impaired IL-2/IL-15 signaling leading to T-cell defects.
- (C) Deficiency in the enzyme adenosine deaminase (ADA): ADA deficiency causes Severe Combined Immunodeficiency (SCID), characterized by accumulation of toxic metabolites (deoxyadenosine) leading to lymphopenia affecting T, B, and NK cells. This is a broader immunodeficiency than described, affecting multiple lineages, and the specific STAT phosphorylation defect is not characteristic.
- (E) Defective production of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ): IFN-γ is crucial for macrophage activation and controlling intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. Defects in IFN-γ production (e.g., due to mutations in *IFNG* or *IFNGR*) or signaling lead to susceptibility to mycobacterial and other intracellular infections. However, this would primarily affect macrophage function and Th1 responses, not necessarily cause the broad T-cell lymphopenia and specific STAT phosphorylation defects described. The vignette points to a defect in T-cell signaling itself, not just IFN-γ production.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | JAK2 Deficiency (Correct) | MHC Class II Deficiency (BLS) | γc Deficiency (X-SCID) | ADA Deficiency (SCID) | IFN-γ Deficiency/Signaling Defect |
|-------------------------|--------------------------|--------------------------------|-------------------------|------------------------|-----------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**      | JAK-STAT signaling       | Antigen Presentation           | γc Receptor Component   | ADA Enzyme             | IFN-γ Production/Signaling        |
| **Affected Cells**      | T cells (CD4+, CD8+)     | CD4+ T cells                   | T cells, NK cells       | T, B, NK cells         | Macrophages, T cells              |
| **Lymphopenia**         | Yes (T cells)            | Severe (CD4+)                  | Severe (T, NK)          | Severe (T, B, NK)      | Variable                         |
| **B cells/Ig**          | Normal                   | Normal                         | Normal                  | Low/Absent             | Normal                           |
| **STAT Phosphorylation**| Impaired (IL-2/IL-15)    | N/A                            | Impaired (IL-2/IL-15)   | N/A                   | N/A                               |
| **Typical Infections**  | TB, Viral                | Viral, Fungal                  | Viral, Fungal, Bacterial| Viral, Fungal, Bacterial| Mycobacterial, Viral             |
| **Inheritance**         | AR                      | AR                             | X-linked                | AR                    | AR/AD                            |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
- High-Yield Takeaway: Defects in the JAK-STAT pathway, particularly involving JAK2, can lead to T-cell lymphopenia and impaired cytokine signaling (IL-2, IL-15), resulting in susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* and recurrent viral infections. Flow cytometry showing impaired STAT phosphorylation upon cytokine stimulation is a key diagnostic clue.
- Differentials: Remember to differentiate JAK-STAT defects from other primary immunodeficiencies affecting T-cell development or function, such as MHC class II deficiency (BLS), γc deficiency (X-SCID), ADA deficiency (SCID), and defects in specific cytokine production or signaling (like IFN-γ). Each has distinct clinical features, affected cell types, and underlying molecular mechanisms.

<a id='question-124'></a>

### Question 124: Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathophysiology of acute Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD), specifically the roles of donor T cells (CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes and CD4+ helper T cells) in recognizing host antigens presented by host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) as foreign, leading to tissue damage. It also assesses the understanding of the key cytokines involved in the inflammatory response during GVHD. (本题考察移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 的发病机制，特别是供者 T 细胞 (CD8+ 细胞毒性 T 淋巴细胞和 CD4+ 辅助 T 细胞) 如何识别宿主抗原呈递细胞 (APCs) 呈递的宿主抗原为外来物，导致组织损伤。同时也考察了 GVHD 过程中炎症反应中涉及的关键细胞因子。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from a matched unrelated donor. His conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, he develops fever, watery diarrhea, and a maculopapular rash starting on his trunk and spreading centrifugally. Liver function tests show elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase. Skin biopsy reveals apoptosis of keratinocytes at the dermoepidermal junction. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD3+ cells, with a predominance of CD8+ T cells. Serum analysis reveals markedly elevated levels of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ) and tumor necrosis factor-alpha (TNF-α).

Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism directly responsible for the observed skin rash and liver injury in this patient?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement fixation by donor antibodies against host endothelial cells.
(B) Direct cytotoxicity by donor natural killer (NK) cells recognizing host cells lacking MHC class I expression.
(C) Recognition of host MHC class II molecules by donor CD4+ T cells, leading to cytokine release and activation of donor CD8+ T cells.
(D) Donor B cells producing antibodies against host red blood cells, causing hemolytic anemia and jaundice.
(E) Donor dendritic cells presenting host antigens to donor CD8+ T cells, leading to direct killing of host epithelial cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen is immunosuppressive but doesn't prevent GVHD, which is a desired effect of the graft's immune system.
- **Symptoms:** Fever, watery diarrhea, maculopapular rash (trunk to periphery). These are classic symptoms of acute GVHD affecting the gut, skin, and liver.
- **Lab Findings:** Elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase indicate liver involvement (hepatitis).
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptosis of keratinocytes at the dermoepidermal junction is characteristic of GVHD skin lesions.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD3+ cells (T cells), predominantly CD8+. This confirms the presence of donor T cells, which are the primary mediators of GVHD.
- **Serum Analysis:** Elevated IFN-γ and TNF-α. These are pro-inflammatory cytokines released by activated T cells, driving the pathology of GVHD.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for skin rash and liver injury.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **GVHD Pathophysiology:** Acute GVHD is primarily mediated by donor T lymphocytes (both CD4+ and CD8+) recognizing host tissues as foreign.
- **Antigen Presentation:** Host antigen-presenting cells (APCs), such as dendritic cells, process and present host antigens (including MHC molecules) to donor T cells.
- **T Cell Activation:** Donor CD4+ T cells recognize host MHC class II molecules presenting host peptides. This recognition, along with co-stimulatory signals, leads to CD4+ T cell activation. Activated CD4+ T cells release cytokines like IFN-γ and TNF-α, which promote inflammation and activate other immune cells, including macrophages and donor CD8+ T cells.
- **Cytotoxicity:** Donor CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I molecules presenting host peptides. This leads to direct cytotoxic killing of host cells expressing these antigens, particularly epithelial cells in the skin, gut, and liver.
- **Cytokine Storm:** The release of large amounts of pro-inflammatory cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6) by activated T cells and other immune cells contributes significantly to the tissue damage and systemic symptoms seen in GVHD.
- **Key Players:** Donor CD4+ T cells (recognize MHC II, release cytokines, help activate CD8+ T cells), Donor CD8+ T cells (recognize MHC I, mediate direct cytotoxicity), Host APCs (present host antigens), Pro-inflammatory cytokines (IFN-γ, TNF-α).

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated complement fixation is characteristic of hyperacute or acute antibody-mediated rejection, not typically the primary mechanism of acute GVHD. While antibodies can play a role, T cells are the main drivers. Donor antibodies against host endothelium would cause vascular damage, but the clinical picture (rash, diarrhea, liver injury) and the specific T cell findings point towards a T cell-mediated process.
- (B): NK cell-mediated cytotoxicity is important in the graft-versus-leukemia effect (killing residual leukemic cells) and can contribute to GVHD, especially in the absence of MHC matching. However, the primary mechanism of acute GVHD involves T cell recognition of host MHC molecules (allogeneic MHC), not necessarily the absence of MHC class I. The predominance of CD8+ T cells in the blood and the cytokine profile strongly favor T cell-mediated GVHD.
- (D): Donor B cells producing antibodies against host red blood cells would cause hemolytic transfusion reactions or hemolytic disease of the fetus and newborn, not the typical symptoms of acute GVHD (rash, diarrhea, hepatitis). While alloantibodies can be produced post-transplant, they are not the primary cause of the described clinical picture.
- (E): While donor dendritic cells can present antigens, the *initiating* event in GVHD is typically the presentation of host antigens by *host* APCs (like dendritic cells in the recipient's tissues) to donor T cells. Donor dendritic cells primarily contribute to graft-versus-leukemia effects or can potentially induce tolerance if modified. The question asks for the mechanism causing the *current* symptoms, which is driven by donor T cell activation against host cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute GVHD (Correct Answer C)                                  | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (Distractor A)                     | NK Cell-Mediated Rejection (Distractor B)                     | Hemolytic Reaction (Distractor D)                               |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Mediator** | Donor T cells (CD4+ & CD8+)                                   | Donor Antibodies & Complement                                  | Donor NK cells                                                  | Donor Antibodies against RBCs                                   |
| **Target Antigen**   | Host MHC I & II molecules presenting host peptides              | Host Endothelial Cell Antigens                                 | Host Cells lacking MHC I or expressing stress ligands          | Host Red Blood Cell Antigens                                   |
| **Key Cytokines**    | IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-2, IL-6                                       | Complement components                                          | IFN-γ (less prominent than in GVHD)                              | N/A                                                              |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Rash, Diarrhea, Hepatitis, Skin/Liver/Gut Damage             | Fever, Hypotension, Vascular Damage, Organ Dysfunction         | Fever, Graft Failure (less common as primary acute rejection) | Fever, Chills, Jaundice, Hemoglobinuria, Renal Failure         |
| **Timing**           | Days to weeks post-allo-HSCT                                    | Minutes to hours (hyperacute), Days to weeks (acute)            | Variable                                                        | Minutes to hours                                                 |
| **Histopathology**   | Keratinocyte apoptosis, Epidermal necrosis, Inflammation        | Endothelialitis, Vascular thrombosis, Fibrinoid necrosis        | Variable, often less specific inflammation                       | N/A (related to RBC destruction)                               |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute GVHD is a major complication of allogeneic HSCT, mediated primarily by donor T cells recognizing host antigens as foreign. The process involves donor CD4+ T cells recognizing host MHC II, releasing cytokines (like IFN-γ and TNF-α), and activating donor CD8+ T cells, which then directly kill host cells expressing MHC I. Key differentials include acute cellular rejection (mediated by donor T cells against graft antigens, not host antigens), antibody-mediated rejection, infection, and drug toxicity. Understanding the specific cell types, cytokines, and target tissues is crucial for diagnosis and management.

<a id='question-125'></a>

### Question 125: Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD) Pathogenesis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis of acute Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD), specifically the role of donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I and class II molecules, leading to tissue damage. (考察移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 的发病机制，特别是供者 T 细胞识别宿主 MHC I 类和 II 类分子，导致组织损伤的过程。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor. His conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, he develops fever, maculopapular rash, and watery diarrhea starting on day 18. Liver function tests show elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase. Skin biopsy reveals apoptosis of keratinocytes at the dermoepidermal junction. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood shows a significant increase in CD8+ T cells expressing the activation marker CD69. Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism underlying the patient's condition?

(A) Donor B cells producing antibodies against host red blood cells, causing hemolytic anemia.
(B) Donor NK cells recognizing host cells lacking MHC class I expression due to viral infection.
(C) Donor CD4+ and CD8+ T cells recognizing host MHC class I and class II molecules, respectively, leading to cytokine release and cytotoxicity.
(D) Recipient antibodies against donor HLA antigens, causing rejection of the transplanted stem cells.
(E) Donor dendritic cells presenting host antigens to recipient T cells, leading to a rejection response against the graft.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 55-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen is immunosuppressive but does not eliminate all recipient immune cells or prevent GVHD.
- **Timeline:** Symptoms (rash, diarrhea, fever, liver dysfunction) start on day 18 post-transplant. This timing is characteristic of acute GVHD (typically occurring within the first 100 days).
- **Symptoms:** Maculopapular rash, watery diarrhea, fever, and elevated liver function tests (bilirubin, alkaline phosphatase). These are classic signs of acute GVHD affecting the skin, gut, and liver, respectively.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptosis of keratinocytes at the dermoepidermal junction. This histological finding is characteristic of GVHD affecting the skin.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased CD8+ T cells expressing CD69. CD69 is an early activation marker for T cells, indicating recent activation of donor T cells. The increase in CD8+ T cells points towards a cytotoxic T cell-mediated process.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism. The combination of clinical presentation, timing, histology, and immunophenotyping strongly points towards acute GVHD mediated by donor T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **GVHD Pathogenesis:** GVHD occurs when immunocompetent donor T cells (primarily CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells) present in the allogeneic graft recognize the recipient's (host) tissues as foreign. This recognition is mediated by differences in MHC molecules (HLA in humans) between the donor and recipient.
- **MHC Recognition:** Donor CD4+ T cells recognize host MHC class II molecules presented by host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells, macrophages, and B cells. Donor CD8+ T cells recognize host MHC class I molecules expressed on virtually all nucleated host cells.
- **T Cell Activation:** Recognition of foreign MHC molecules triggers T cell receptor (TCR) signaling, leading to T cell activation, proliferation, and differentiation. Early activation is marked by upregulation of markers like CD69.
- **Effector Mechanisms:** Activated donor T cells release pro-inflammatory cytokines (e.g., IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-17) and exert cytotoxic effects (primarily by CD8+ T cells) against host tissues. This leads to inflammation and damage in target organs like the skin, liver, and gastrointestinal tract.
- **Acute vs. Chronic GVHD:** Acute GVHD typically occurs within the first 100 days and primarily affects the skin, liver, and gut. Chronic GVHD occurs later (>100 days) and can affect multiple organs, often with features resembling autoimmune diseases. The vignette describes classic signs of acute GVHD.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Donor B cells producing antibodies against host red blood cells, causing hemolytic anemia. This describes a form of transfusion reaction or hemolytic disease of the newborn, not GVHD. While donor B cells can contribute to GVHD, the primary mechanism involves T cell recognition of MHC antigens, not antibody-mediated hemolysis. GVHD typically involves skin, liver, and gut, not primarily hemolysis.
- (B): Donor NK cells recognizing host cells lacking MHC class I expression due to viral infection. NK cells are part of the innate immune system and can mediate graft rejection (especially in the absence of HLA matching) or contribute to GVHD, but their primary mechanism is recognizing cells lacking MHC class I (missing-self hypothesis) or cells expressing stress ligands. While NK cells can play a role, the classic acute GVHD pathogenesis involves T cell recognition of host MHC I and II, and the vignette points strongly to T cell activation (CD69+ CD8+ T cells). Viral infection causing MHC I downregulation is not the primary driver of GVHD itself.
- (D): Recipient antibodies against donor HLA antigens, causing rejection of the transplanted stem cells. This describes graft rejection, where the recipient's immune system attacks the donor graft. In GVHD, the *donor's* immune cells attack the *recipient's* tissues. The patient has received the graft, and the symptoms are consistent with the graft attacking the host, not the host rejecting the graft.
- (E): Donor dendritic cells presenting host antigens to recipient T cells, leading to a rejection response against the graft. This describes a scenario where donor APCs prime recipient T cells against host antigens, potentially leading to graft rejection. However, in GVHD, the *donor T cells* are the primary effectors recognizing *host* MHC molecules. While donor APCs are involved in initiating the response, the effector cells are donor T cells attacking the host. This option incorrectly describes the direction of the immune attack.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD)                                  | Graft Rejection                                        | Hemolytic Transfusion Reaction                          |
|----------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| **Immune Cells**     | Donor T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                                         | Recipient T cells (CD4+, CD8+), Antibodies              | Recipient Antibodies (IgM/IgG) against donor RBCs      |
| **Target**           | Recipient (Host) tissues                                           | Donor graft cells                                       | Donor Red Blood Cells                                  |
| **Antigen Recognition**| Donor T cells recognize Host MHC I & II                             | Recipient T cells recognize Donor MHC I & II             | Recipient Abs recognize Donor RBC antigens (e.g., ABO) |
| **Timing (Acute)**   | Typically days to weeks post-transplant (e.g., day 18)               | Days to weeks post-transplant                             | Minutes to hours post-transfusion                       |
| **Clinical Manifestations**| Skin rash, diarrhea, liver dysfunction (skin, liver, gut)        | Fever, graft failure, organ dysfunction                 | Fever, chills, jaundice, hemoglobinuria                |
| **Key Cells Involved**| Donor T cells, Host APCs presenting host MHC                       | Recipient T cells, Donor APCs presenting donor MHC      | Recipient B cells/Plasma cells                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
GVHD is a major complication of allogeneic HSCT where donor T cells attack recipient tissues due to MHC disparity. Differentiating GVHD from graft rejection (host attacks graft) is crucial. GVHD typically presents with skin, liver, and gut involvement, driven by donor T cell activation. Key differentials include infection, drug toxicity, and graft rejection. Remember the direction of the immune attack: Donor vs. Host in GVHD, Host vs. Donor in graft rejection.

<a id='question-126'></a>

### Question 126: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 26 (INNATE)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (specifically the alternative pathway) in innate immunity and its dysregulation in certain diseases, including atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS) caused by mutations in complement regulatory proteins. (理解补体激活（特别是替代途径）在先天免疫中的作用及其在某些疾病中的失调，包括由补体调节蛋白突变引起的非典型溶血性尿毒症综合征 (aHUS)。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy with a history of recurrent upper respiratory infections presents to the emergency department with acute onset of bloody diarrhea, microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA) characterized by schistocytes on peripheral blood smear, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury (AKI) with oliguria. His mother reports he recently had a diarrheal illness preceded by a fever. Laboratory studies show elevated creatinine (3.0 mg/dL), low haptoglobin, elevated LDH, and a negative direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test). A renal biopsy reveals thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA) with fibrin deposition in arterioles but no significant immune complex deposition. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *CFH* gene (complement factor H).

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to this patient's condition?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to C3 deposition on endothelial cells.
(B) Defective phagocytosis of apoptotic neutrophils by macrophages due to impaired scavenger receptors.
(C) Uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway on endothelial surfaces.
(D) Impaired opsonization of bacteria by complement components leading to recurrent infections.
(E) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to mutations in late complement components.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent infections. This suggests a potential underlying immune defect.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Acute bloody diarrhea, MAHA (schistocytes), thrombocytopenia, AKI (oliguria). This triad is characteristic of thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA).
*   **Recent History:** Diarrheal illness preceded by fever. This often triggers TMA, particularly Shiga toxin-producing *E. coli* (STEC)-HUS, but the negative DAT makes typical STEC-HUS less likely (though not impossible).
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated creatinine (AKI), low haptoglobin, elevated LDH (hemolysis), negative DAT (rules out autoimmune hemolytic anemia). These confirm MAHA and kidney injury.
*   **Renal Biopsy:** TMA with fibrin deposition, no significant immune complex deposition. This points away from immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis and towards a primary TMA process.
*   **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *CFH* gene. Complement factor H (CFH) is a key regulator of the alternative complement pathway. Mutations in *CFH* are strongly associated with atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the underlying mechanism. Given the clinical picture of TMA, negative DAT, and *CFH* mutation, the mechanism involves dysregulated complement activation.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization, inflammation, and direct lysis of pathogens (via MAC). It has three activation pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), lectin (mannose-binding lectin-dependent), and alternative (spontaneous activation on pathogen/abnormal surfaces).
*   **Alternative Pathway:** This pathway is continuously active at a low level. C3 spontaneously hydrolyzes to C3(H2O), which binds Factor B. Factor D cleaves Factor B into Ba and Bb, forming the C3 convertase (C3bBb). C3bBb cleaves more C3 into C3a and C3b, amplifying the cascade. This amplification occurs preferentially on surfaces lacking complement regulatory proteins or possessing activating molecules.
*   **Complement Regulation:** Host cells express complement regulatory proteins (e.g., Factor H, Factor I, CD55/DAF, CD59/MIRL) to prevent excessive complement activation on self-surfaces. Factor H binds C3bBb, accelerating its dissociation, and acts as a cofactor for Factor I, which cleaves C3b into inactive iC3b.
*   **aHUS and CFH:** Mutations in *CFH* impair its ability to regulate the alternative pathway. Reduced Factor H function leads to uncontrolled C3bBb formation on endothelial surfaces, particularly in the renal microvasculature. This results in excessive C3 activation, generation of C5a (anaphylatoxin causing inflammation), and formation of C5b-9 (MAC), leading to endothelial damage, platelet aggregation, fibrin deposition, and TMA.
*   **Distinguishing aHUS from Typical HUS:** Typical HUS (often caused by STEC) involves Shiga toxin damaging endothelial cells, leading to TMA. While complement activation can occur secondarily, it's not the primary driver. aHUS, especially with *CFH* mutations, is a primary dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway. The negative DAT in this case also makes autoimmune hemolytic anemia less likely, further supporting a primary TMA process like aHUS.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to C3 deposition on endothelial cells. This describes the classical or lectin pathway activation triggered by antibodies or microbial lectins. While complement deposition occurs in aHUS, the primary defect in this patient (CFH mutation) affects the *alternative* pathway, not antibody-mediated activation. The negative DAT also argues against significant antibody involvement.
- (B) Defective phagocytosis of apoptotic neutrophils by macrophages due to impaired scavenger receptors. This mechanism is associated with systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE), where impaired clearance of apoptotic cells leads to the accumulation of nuclear antigens and autoantibody production. It is not the primary mechanism of aHUS.
- (D) Impaired opsonization of bacteria by complement components leading to recurrent infections. While complement (especially C3b) is crucial for opsonization, the primary manifestation of CFH deficiency is TMA due to endothelial damage, not primarily impaired bacterial clearance leading to recurrent infections (although some increased susceptibility might occur). The main clinical feature presented is TMA, not overwhelming infection.
- (E) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to mutations in late complement components. Mutations in late complement components (C5-C9) impair MAC formation, leading to increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections (e.g., meningococcal disease). This is the opposite of what happens in aHUS, where excessive complement activation and MAC formation contribute to endothelial damage.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Atypical HUS (aHUS) with CFH mutation | Typical HUS (STEC-HUS) | Autoimmune Hemolytic Anemia (AIHA) | SLE                                  | Neisserial Infections (Late Complement Def.) |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------------------ | :-------------------- | :---------------------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------ |
| **Primary Defect**      | Uncontrolled Alternative Complement   | Shiga Toxin Damage   | Autoantibodies against RBCs        | Impaired clearance of apoptotic cells | Defective MAC formation (C5-C9)             |
| **Complement Role**     | Excessive Activation                  | Secondary Activation  | Antibody-mediated activation       | Activation by immune complexes       | Impaired lysis of bacteria                 |
| **Key Gene**            | *CFH*, *CFI*, *MCP*, *C3*              | N/A                  | N/A                                 | N/A                                  | *C5-C9*                                     |
| **Clinical Triad**      | MAHA, Thrombocytopenia, AKI           | MAHA, Thrombocytopenia, AKI | Anemia, Jaundice, Splenomegaly     | Multisystem (rash, arthritis, nephritis) | Recurrent *Neisseria* meningitis/gonorrhea |
| **DAT (Coombs Test)**   | Negative                              | Negative              | Positive                            | Often Positive                       | Negative                                    |
| **Renal Biopsy**        | TMA, Fibrin Deposition                | TMA                  | N/A                                 | Glomerulonephritis (various types)    | N/A                                         |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing that mutations in complement regulatory proteins, particularly Factor H (*CFH*), lead to uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway, causing endothelial damage and TMA, characteristic of atypical HUS (aHUS). Differentiating aHUS from typical HUS (Shiga toxin-mediated) and other causes of TMA (like TTP or AIHA) requires understanding the underlying pathophysiology and diagnostic clues like DAT results and genetic testing.

<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 27 (IMMUNE)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (specifically the alternative pathway) in innate immunity and its interaction with adaptive immunity, particularly in the context of bacterial infections and immune complex formation. (理解补体激活（特别是替代途径）在先天免疫中的作用及其与适应性免疫的相互作用，特别是在细菌感染和免疫复合物形成的情况下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic kidney disease (CKD) on hemodialysis presents to the emergency department with fever, chills, and flank pain. He reports feeling unwell for the past 3 days. Physical examination reveals a temperature of 39.2°C, tachycardia (110 bpm), and tenderness over the right flank. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis (18,000/µL) with neutrophilia, elevated C-reactive protein (CRP), and evidence of acute kidney injury (AKI) superimposed on his CKD. Urinalysis reveals pyuria and bacteriuria. A urine culture grows *Escherichia coli*. Blood cultures are pending. The patient is started on empiric intravenous antibiotics (ceftriaxone). Despite antibiotic therapy, the patient develops worsening sepsis, characterized by hypotension (systolic BP 85 mmHg) and altered mental status. Further investigation reveals markedly decreased serum C3 and C4 levels, and a positive direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test) on his red blood cells. A renal biopsy shows diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial immune complex deposits.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely contributing to the patient's worsening sepsis and glomerulonephritis?

(A) Activation of the classical complement pathway by antibodies bound to bacterial surface antigens, leading to opsonization and phagocytosis, but also immune complex deposition.
(B) Activation of the lectin pathway of complement by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) binding to bacterial cell wall components, resulting in C3 convertase formation and subsequent inflammation.
(C) Activation of the alternative complement pathway by bacterial cell surface lipopolysaccharide (LPS) binding directly to Factor B, leading to C3b deposition on bacteria and endothelial cells, promoting inflammation and immune complex formation.
(D) Inhibition of the alternative complement pathway by Factor H binding to bacterial surface antigens, preventing C3b deposition and opsonization, thus allowing bacterial proliferation and dissemination.
(E) Formation of membrane attack complex (MAC) by all complement pathways, leading to direct bacterial lysis, but also causing endothelial damage and contributing to AKI.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with CKD on hemodialysis. CKD and hemodialysis are risk factors for infections and immune dysregulation.
- **Presentation:** Fever, chills, flank pain, leukocytosis, neutrophilia, elevated CRP, AKI, pyuria, bacteriuria (*E. coli*). This points towards a urinary tract infection (UTI) complicated by sepsis and AKI.
- **Worsening Sepsis:** Despite antibiotics, the patient develops hypotension and altered mental status, indicating severe sepsis or septic shock.
- **Immunological Findings:** Decreased C3 and C4 levels suggest complement consumption. Positive DAT indicates antibody-mediated hemolysis (immune hemolytic anemia).
- **Renal Biopsy:** Diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis with subepithelial immune complex deposits suggests immune complex-mediated kidney damage.
- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has a severe bacterial infection (*E. coli*), leading to sepsis, AKI, complement consumption (low C3/C4), immune complex formation (glomerulonephritis, positive DAT), and hemolysis. The question asks for the mechanism contributing to these findings.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization (C3b), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and direct lysis (MAC). It has three activation pathways: classical, lectin, and alternative.
- **Classical Pathway:** Activated by antibody-antigen complexes (IgG or IgM). C1q binds to the Fc region of antibodies. This pathway consumes C1, C4, and C2.
- **Lectin Pathway:** Activated by mannose-binding lectin (MBL) or ficolins binding to microbial carbohydrates. MBL-associated serine proteases (MASPs) cleave C4 and C2. This pathway consumes C4 and C2.
- **Alternative Pathway:** Activated spontaneously at a low level on host cells or directly by microbial surfaces (e.g., LPS, zymosan, fungal cell walls) without antibodies. Factor B binds to C3b deposited on the surface. Factor D cleaves Factor B into Ba and Bb, forming the C3 convertase (C3bBb). This pathway consumes Factor B, Factor D, and C3. It is amplified by properdin.
- **Role in Sepsis:** Bacterial infections, especially Gram-negative infections like *E. coli* (LPS), strongly activate the alternative complement pathway. LPS directly binds Factor B, initiating the cascade.
- **Role in Immune Complexes:** Complement activation (all pathways converge at C3) leads to C3b deposition. C3b opsonizes bacteria for phagocytosis. C3b also binds to immune complexes, facilitating their clearance. However, excessive immune complex formation can lead to deposition in tissues (like glomeruli), causing inflammation and damage (glomerulonephritis).
- **Role in Hemolysis:** Complement activation can lead to MAC formation on red blood cells, causing hemolysis. In this case, the positive DAT suggests antibody-mediated hemolysis, potentially exacerbated by complement activation.
- **Connecting to the Case:** The patient has *E. coli* sepsis. *E. coli* LPS is a potent activator of the alternative pathway. This activation leads to:
    - C3b deposition on bacteria (opsonization) and endothelial cells (inflammation).
    - Formation of C3 convertase (C3bBb) and C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b).
    - Consumption of complement components (low C3, C4 - C4 is also consumed in classical/lectin pathways, which can be secondarily activated by immune complexes).
    - Formation of C5a (inflammation, chemotaxis) and C3a (inflammation).
    - Formation of MAC (potential cell damage).
    - Immune complex formation (antigen-antibody-C3b complexes) leading to glomerulonephritis and potentially hemolysis (positive DAT).
- **Why Alternative Pathway is Key:** The alternative pathway is the primary initiator in response to direct bacterial surfaces like LPS, even before antibodies are produced. Its activation explains the rapid complement consumption and inflammatory response seen in sepsis. The subsequent formation of immune complexes can activate the classical pathway as well, contributing to C4 consumption.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): While the classical pathway is involved in immune complex formation and opsonization, it requires pre-existing antibodies. The alternative pathway is the primary initiator in response to direct bacterial surfaces like LPS, especially early in infection. The vignette emphasizes the direct bacterial infection (*E. coli*) as the trigger for sepsis and complement consumption, making the alternative pathway the most likely primary driver. The low C4 also suggests involvement beyond just the classical pathway, although secondary activation is possible.
- (B): The lectin pathway is activated by specific microbial carbohydrates. While *E. coli* has carbohydrates, LPS is a much more potent activator of the alternative pathway. MBL deficiency is a known immunodeficiency, but the scenario describes a typical response to bacterial sepsis, not a deficiency.
- (D): Factor H is a *regulator* of the alternative pathway. It binds to C3b and promotes its inactivation by Factor I, preventing excessive complement activation on host cells. Inhibition by Factor H would *prevent* opsonization and inflammation, leading to *worse* bacterial control, not worsening sepsis. This option describes a mechanism that would protect against complement-mediated damage, the opposite of what is observed.
- (E): MAC formation is a final common pathway for all complement pathways. While it contributes to bacterial lysis and can cause collateral damage (like AKI), it doesn't fully explain the immune complex formation (glomerulonephritis, positive DAT) and the *initiation* of the cascade in response to LPS. The alternative pathway is the primary initiator triggered by the bacterial surface, leading to the subsequent steps including MAC formation and immune complex deposition. Option C better describes the *initial* and *dominant* mechanism driving the overall clinical picture in this context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Alternative Pathway (Correct) | Classical Pathway (Distractor A) | Lectin Pathway (Distractor B) | Factor H Inhibition (Distractor D) | MAC Formation (Distractor E) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------------------------|------------------------------|
| **Initiation Trigger** | Direct microbial surfaces (LPS, zymosan), spontaneous hydrolysis of C3 | Antibody-antigen complexes (IgG, IgM) | Microbial carbohydrates (mannose) | N/A (Regulatory)                | N/A (Final step)             |
| **Key Components**   | Factor B, Factor D, Properdin    | C1q, C1r, C1s, C4, C2            | MBL, MASPs, C4, C2             | Factor H, Factor I                  | C5b, C6, C7, C8, C9          |
| **Consumption**      | C3, Factor B, Factor D          | C1, C4, C2                        | C4, C2                          | N/A (Prevents consumption)          | C5, C6, C7, C8, C9           |
| **Role in Sepsis**   | Primary initiator by LPS        | Secondary activation by complexes | Less significant for LPS        | Prevents inflammation               | Contributes to lysis/damage  |
| **Role in Glomerulonephritis** | Immune complex formation     | Immune complex formation         | Immune complex formation         | Prevents complex deposition        | Contributes to tissue damage |
| **Relevance to Case** | *E. coli* LPS trigger          | Requires pre-formed antibodies   | Less direct role with LPS        | Opposite effect observed            | Final step, not initiator    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The alternative complement pathway is the primary innate immune response to bacterial surfaces like LPS, initiating inflammation, opsonization, and contributing to immune complex formation. Understanding the triggers and components of each complement pathway is crucial for diagnosing conditions like sepsis, glomerulonephritis, and immune deficiencies. Differentiate alternative pathway activation (direct bacterial trigger) from classical pathway activation (antibody-dependent). Remember that complement consumption (low C3/C4) and immune complex deposition are common features in severe bacterial infections.

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Immunodeficiency and Opportunistic Infection
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of specific immunodeficiencies, particularly those affecting T-cell function and cytokine signaling, and their association with susceptibility to specific opportunistic infections. It also assesses the ability to interpret clinical presentation and laboratory findings to identify the underlying immune defect. (本题考察特定免疫缺陷的理解，特别是影响T细胞功能和细胞因子信号传导的缺陷，以及它们与特定机会性感染的易感性之间的关联。它还评估了根据临床表现和实验室结果来识别潜在免疫缺陷的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male with a history of recurrent severe bacterial and fungal infections since childhood presents with a 3-week history of progressive dyspnea, non-productive cough, and fever. He was diagnosed with severe combined immunodeficiency (SCID) at age 2 and underwent a hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor 5 years ago. Post-transplant, he developed chronic graft-versus-host disease (cGVHD), which is currently managed with immunosuppressive therapy including cyclosporine and methotrexate. Physical examination reveals diffuse bilateral interstitial infiltrates on chest auscultation and generalized lymphadenopathy. A bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows numerous spherical, thick-walled organisms with internal septations, measuring 5-10 μm in diameter, visualized with Gomori methenamine silver (GMS) stain. Cultures of the BAL fluid grow a fungus that is resistant to amphotericin B and fluconazole. The patient's post-transplant course has also been complicated by chronic diarrhea and weight loss. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood mononuclear cells (PBMCs) reveals a markedly reduced number of CD4+ T cells (150 cells/μL) and normal numbers of CD8+ T cells, B cells, and NK cells.

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to this patient's current opportunistic infection?

(A) Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion due to mutations in *LYST* gene.
(B) Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene.
(C) Defective interleukin-7 receptor (IL-7R) signaling leading to impaired T-cell development.
(D) Defective Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) signaling impairing cytokine receptor signaling.
(E) Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient History:** 35-year-old male with SCID, HSCT, cGVHD on immunosuppression (cyclosporine, methotrexate). This history points towards a complex immune status involving both a primary immunodeficiency (SCID) and secondary immunosuppression.
*   **Presenting Symptoms:** Progressive dyspnea, cough, fever for 3 weeks. Suggests a pulmonary infection.
*   **Physical Exam:** Bilateral interstitial infiltrates, generalized lymphadenopathy. Consistent with an atypical pneumonia or disseminated infection.
*   **BAL Findings:** Spherical, thick-walled organisms with internal septations (5-10 μm) visualized with GMS stain. This morphology is characteristic of *Pneumocystis jirovecii* (formerly *Pneumocystis carinii*).
*   **Culture Results:** Fungus resistant to amphotericin B and fluconazole. *Pneumocystis jirovecii* is not typically cultured using standard fungal media; however, the resistance profile is consistent with the organism's known resistance patterns to common antifungals. The description likely refers to the organism's resistance to standard treatments, which are often ineffective.
*   **Chronic Symptoms:** Chronic diarrhea and weight loss. Common manifestations of opportunistic infections in immunocompromised patients, including *Pneumocystis* pneumonia (PCP).
*   **Flow Cytometry:** Markedly reduced CD4+ T cells (150 cells/μL). This severe T-cell lymphopenia is the key immunological finding. Normal CD8+, B, and NK cells suggest a selective T-cell defect, likely related to the underlying SCID or exacerbated by immunosuppression.
*   **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has severe CD4+ T-cell lymphopenia, a history of SCID, and is on immunosuppression. He develops *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PCP), a classic opportunistic infection associated with profound T-cell deficiency, particularly affecting CD4+ T cells. The question asks for the *underlying mechanism* contributing to this susceptibility.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **SCID and T-cell Defects:** SCID encompasses a group of genetic disorders characterized by severe defects in T-cell development and/or function. Common forms include X-linked SCID (due to *IL2RG* mutations affecting common gamma chain of IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21 receptors), ADA deficiency, RAG deficiency, and JAK3 deficiency.
*   ***Pneumocystis jirovecii* Pathogenesis:** *P. jirovecii* is an opportunistic fungus that primarily infects the lungs of immunocompromised individuals, especially those with impaired cell-mediated immunity (CD4+ T cells). The pathogenesis involves adherence to alveolar epithelial cells, followed by invasion and proliferation. Effective clearance requires functional CD4+ T cells, which orchestrate the immune response involving macrophages and other cells.
*   **JAK3 Signaling:** Janus kinase 3 (JAK3) is a tyrosine kinase essential for signaling through several cytokine receptors crucial for lymphocyte development and function, including the common gamma chain receptors (IL-2, IL-4, IL-7, IL-9, IL-15, IL-21) and the IL-6 receptor. Mutations in *JAK3* cause autosomal recessive SCID, characterized by a profound lack of T cells and NK cells, and often B cells as well, due to defective signaling required for their development and survival.
*   **Link to the Case:** The patient has a history of SCID and severe CD4+ T-cell lymphopenia. While the specific type of SCID isn't stated, the profound T-cell defect makes him highly susceptible to PCP. The question asks for the *underlying mechanism*. Given the options, defective JAK3 signaling (Option D) is a known cause of SCID leading to severe T-cell deficiency, directly explaining the susceptibility to *P. jirovecii*. The immunosuppression from cGVHD treatment further exacerbates the T-cell deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective phagosome-lysosome fusion due to mutations in *LYST* gene:** This describes Chediak-Higashi syndrome, an autosomal recessive disorder affecting phagocytes (neutrophils, macrophages). It leads to recurrent pyogenic infections (often *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Serratia marcescens*), partial oculocutaneous albinism, and neurological abnormalities. While patients are susceptible to infections, the primary defect is in phagocyte function, not T-cell development, and the characteristic infections differ from PCP. The morphology described in the BAL is not typical for bacteria associated with Chediak-Higashi.
- (B) **Impaired T-cell receptor (TCR) signaling due to mutations in the *ZAP70* gene:** Mutations in *ZAP70* cause SCID characterized by a block in T-cell and NK-cell development, but B-cell numbers are usually normal. While this leads to T-cell deficiency and susceptibility to PCP, JAK3 deficiency (Option D) is a more common cause of SCID presenting with severe T-cell lymphopenia and is a plausible underlying mechanism given the patient's history. Furthermore, JAK3 deficiency affects signaling through multiple cytokine receptors critical for T-cell development, making it a broader defect than just TCR signaling.
- (C) **Defective interleukin-7 receptor (IL-7R) signaling leading to impaired T-cell development:** Mutations in the *IL7R* gene (encoding the IL-7Rα chain, CD127) cause SCID characterized by severe T-cell lymphopenia, often with normal or increased B-cell numbers. This is a valid cause of SCID and would predispose to PCP. However, JAK3 deficiency (Option D) is also a common cause of SCID with severe T-cell deficiency and affects signaling through the IL-7R complex (as it requires JAK3 for signaling), as well as other critical cytokine receptors. Given the options, JAK3 is a more encompassing defect affecting multiple pathways crucial for T-cell development and function.
- (E) **Defective complement component C3 leading to impaired opsonization:** C3 deficiency is a rare primary immunodeficiency affecting the complement system. It leads to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*, *Neisseria meningitidis*) due to impaired opsonization and bacterial lysis. It does not primarily affect T-cell function or predispose to PCP.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Patient Case (SCID + cGVHD) | Chediak-Higashi (LYST) | ZAP70 SCID | IL-7R SCID | C3 Deficiency |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------- | :---------------------- | :--------- | :--------- | :------------ |
| Primary Defect          | Severe T-cell lymphopenia   | Phagocyte function      | T/NK cell development | T-cell development | Complement |
| Key Gene                | Likely JAK3 (or other SCID) | *LYST*                  | *ZAP70*    | *IL7R*     | *C3*          |
| Characteristic Infections | PCP, opportunistic fungi, viruses | Pyogenic bacteria     | PCP, viruses | PCP, viruses | Encapsulated bacteria |
| BAL Finding             | *Pneumocystis jirovecii*    | Bacteria                | N/A        | N/A        | N/A           |
| CD4+ T cells            | Severely reduced           | Normal                  | Severely reduced | Severely reduced | Normal        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Severe CD4+ T-cell lymphopenia is the hallmark of susceptibility to *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PCP). This patient's history of SCID, coupled with immunosuppression for cGVHD, explains the profound T-cell deficiency. Defects in JAK3 signaling are a common cause of SCID leading to severe T-cell deficiency and increased risk for PCP. Differentiating SCID subtypes requires specific genetic testing, but understanding the functional consequences (e.g., severe T-cell deficiency) is crucial for linking the immune defect to opportunistic infections. Key differentials for severe T-cell deficiency include HIV infection, advanced malignancy, and immunosuppressive therapy, but the history of SCID points strongly towards a primary genetic defect.

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD) Pathogenesis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the pathogenesis of acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), specifically the role of donor T cells recognizing host antigens presented by host antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and the key cytokines involved in the inflammatory cascade. (考察移植物抗宿主病 (GVHD) 的发病机制，特别是供者 T 细胞识别由宿主抗原提呈细胞 (APCs) 呈递的宿主抗原的作用，以及炎症级联反应中涉及的关键细胞因子。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from a matched unrelated donor. His conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, he develops fever, maculopapular rash, and watery diarrhea starting on day 18. Skin biopsy reveals apoptosis of keratinocytes and inflammatory infiltrate. Flow cytometry of peripheral blood shows a significant increase in donor-derived CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. Serum analysis reveals elevated levels of IL-1, IL-6, TNF-α, and IFN-γ. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the initial activation of donor T cells leading to this clinical presentation?

(A) Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class II molecules on donor-derived dendritic cells presenting self-peptides.
(B) Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules on host dendritic cells presenting self-peptides.
(C) Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class II molecules on host dendritic cells presenting self-peptides.
(D) Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules on donor-derived dendritic cells presenting self-peptides.
(E) Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class II molecules on host dendritic cells presenting foreign peptides derived from the conditioning regimen.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen aims to ablate the patient's own hematopoietic system and suppress immune function, but it doesn't eliminate all APCs.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, maculopapular rash, watery diarrhea starting on day 18 post-transplant. This is the classic triad of acute GVHD. The timing (day 18) is typical for acute GVHD.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptosis of keratinocytes and inflammatory infiltrate. This is characteristic histopathology of GVHD skin involvement.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Increased donor-derived CD4+ and CD8+ T cells. Confirms the presence and expansion of donor lymphocytes, the effector cells in GVHD.
- **Serum Analysis:** Elevated IL-1, IL-6, TNF-α, IFN-γ. These are pro-inflammatory cytokines characteristic of the GVHD inflammatory response.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of *initial activation* of donor T cells. This points to the antigen presentation event that triggers the T cell response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **GVHD Pathogenesis:** Acute GVHD is primarily mediated by donor T cells recognizing host antigens as foreign.
- **Antigen Presentation:** Host antigen-presenting cells (APCs), particularly dendritic cells (DCs), survive the conditioning regimen. These host DCs process host self-peptides and present them on MHC molecules.
- **T Cell Recognition:** Donor T cells, which have proliferated post-transplant, recognize these host self-peptides presented by host MHC molecules on host APCs.
- **MHC Restriction:** CD4+ T cells (helper T cells) recognize peptides presented on MHC class II molecules. CD8+ T cells (cytotoxic T cells) recognize peptides presented on MHC class I molecules. Both CD4+ and CD8+ T cells contribute to acute GVHD.
- **Cytokine Release:** Upon recognition of the antigen-MHC complex, T cells become activated and release cytokines like IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-1, and IL-6, driving the inflammatory cascade that damages host tissues (skin, gut, liver).
- **Key Point:** The *initial* activation requires donor T cells to encounter host antigens presented by host APCs. The antigens are self-peptides (derived from the patient's tissues), not foreign peptides from the conditioning regimen (which are usually cleared or not presented in a way that triggers GVHD). The APCs are primarily host-derived, not donor-derived, because the conditioning regimen aims to eliminate the recipient's immune system but often leaves some host APCs intact, which then present host antigens.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class II molecules on *donor-derived* dendritic cells presenting self-peptides. Incorrect. While donor DCs might be present, the primary APCs initiating GVHD are host DCs presenting host antigens. Donor DCs would present donor self-peptides, which would typically lead to tolerance or rejection of the graft, not GVHD.
- (B): Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules on host dendritic cells presenting self-peptides. Partially correct but incomplete. CD8+ T cells do recognize host MHC I presenting self-peptides, contributing to GVHD. However, CD4+ T cells also play a crucial role, recognizing host MHC II. Option (C) is more comprehensive as it includes the recognition by CD4+ T cells, which are critical orchestrators of the GVHD response. The question asks for the *most direct* mechanism, and the recognition of host MHC II by CD4+ T cells is a central initiating event.
- (D): Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I molecules on *donor-derived* dendritic cells presenting self-peptides. Incorrect. Same reasoning as (A). Donor DCs presenting donor self-peptides are not the primary trigger for GVHD.
- (E): Donor T cells recognizing host MHC class II molecules on host dendritic cells presenting *foreign peptides derived from the conditioning regimen*. Incorrect. The conditioning regimen peptides are not the primary antigens driving GVHD. GVHD is triggered by the recognition of host self-antigens (alloantigens) presented by host APCs. While conditioning agents can be immunogenic, they are not the main drivers of acute GVHD pathogenesis.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Acute GVHD Pathogenesis                                  | Graft Rejection (by recipient)                     | Chronic GVHD Pathogenesis                          |
|----------------------|----------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| **Effector Cells**   | Donor T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                               | Recipient T cells (CD4+, CD8+)                     | Donor T cells (often regulatory T cells), B cells |
| **Target**           | Host tissues (skin, gut, liver)                          | Donor graft tissues                                | Host tissues (skin, gut, liver, lungs, etc.)      |
| **Antigen Source**   | Host self-antigens (alloantigens)                        | Donor alloantigens                                 | Host self-antigens (alloantigens), possibly donor |
| **APC Source**       | Primarily Host APCs (e.g., dendritic cells)              | Recipient APCs                                    | Host APCs, possibly donor APCs                     |
| **MHC Restriction**  | Donor T cells recognize Host MHC (Class I & II)           | Recipient T cells recognize Donor MHC (Class I & II) | Donor T cells recognize Host MHC (Class I & II)     |
| **Timing**           | Typically days to weeks post-transplant (e.g., day 18)   | Days to weeks post-transplant                      | Months to years post-transplant                     |
| **Key Cytokines**    | IFN-γ, TNF-α, IL-1, IL-6                                 | IFN-γ, TNF-α                                      | IL-6, IL-10, TGF-β, autoantibodies                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute GVHD is driven by donor T cells recognizing host antigens presented by host APCs. This involves donor T cells recognizing host MHC class I and II molecules presenting self-peptides. Understanding the source of APCs (host vs. donor) and the nature of the antigens (self vs. foreign) is crucial. Differentiate GVHD from graft rejection (recipient T cells attacking donor MHC) and chronic GVHD (different mechanisms, often involving autoimmunity and fibrosis).
