<a id='question-144'></a>

### Question 144: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 44 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (classical, alternative, lectin pathways) and its regulation, specifically focusing on the function of complement regulatory proteins like Factor H and its deficiency leading to uncontrolled complement activation and associated diseases. (理解补体激活途径及其调控，特别是Factor H的功能及其缺乏导致的失控激活和相关疾病。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe lower abdominal pain, bloody diarrhea, and fever. He reports a history of recurrent urinary tract infections since childhood and a recent episode of glomerulonephritis requiring hospitalization. Family history is significant for a similar presentation in his maternal uncle, who died in his 40s from kidney failure. Laboratory findings show elevated C3 and C4 levels, decreased Factor H activity, and microscopic hematuria with proteinuria. A renal biopsy reveals membranoproliferative glomerulonephritis (MPGN).

Which of the following is the most likely underlying mechanism contributing to this patient's recurrent infections and glomerulonephritis?

(A) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to C5b-9 deficiency.
(B) Impaired opsonization by C3b due to deficiency in C3 convertase.
(C) Uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway on host cells due to insufficient regulation.
(D) Inability to initiate the classical complement pathway due to deficiency in C1q.
(E) Failure to activate the lectin pathway due to mannose-binding lectin (MBL) deficiency.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Presentation:** 32-year-old male with acute abdominal pain, bloody diarrhea, fever, recurrent UTIs, recent glomerulonephritis, family history of similar illness and kidney failure. This suggests a systemic inflammatory condition affecting multiple organ systems, potentially related to immune dysregulation.
*   **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated C3 and C4, decreased Factor H activity, hematuria, proteinuria. Elevated C3/C4 indicates complement consumption is *not* the primary issue; rather, the *regulation* is impaired. Decreased Factor H activity is a direct clue pointing towards a defect in alternative pathway regulation. Hematuria/proteinuria and renal biopsy showing MPGN are classic signs of complement-mediated kidney damage.
*   **Family History:** Suggests a genetic basis for the condition.
*   **Connecting Clues:** The combination of recurrent infections (especially UTI, suggesting impaired clearance of pathogens), glomerulonephritis (MPGN), elevated C3/C4 (consumption is low), and specifically decreased Factor H activity strongly points to dysregulation of the alternative complement pathway.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of protein activation leading to opsonization (C3b), inflammation (C3a, C5a), and direct lysis (MAC, C5b-9). There are three main activation pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), lectin (mannose-binding lectin/ficolin-dependent), and alternative (spontaneous activation on pathogen surfaces).
*   **Alternative Pathway:** This pathway is continuously activated at a low level ("tick-over") on microbial surfaces and some host cell surfaces. It is tightly regulated by host cell proteins to prevent damage to self-tissues. Key regulators include Factor H, Factor I, C4bp, and MCP (CD46). Factor H binds to C3b and inhibits C3 convertase (C3bBb) activity and promotes its degradation by Factor I.
*   **Factor H Deficiency:** Factor H is a soluble glycoprotein that acts as the primary regulator of the alternative pathway. Genetic mutations in Factor H can lead to decreased levels or function. Without sufficient Factor H, C3bBb (C3 convertase) accumulates on host cell surfaces (especially endothelial cells, podocytes in the kidney), leading to excessive C3 cleavage, C3b deposition, and subsequent formation of C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b) and MAC (C5b-9).
*   **Clinical Manifestations of Factor H Deficiency:** Uncontrolled alternative pathway activation causes chronic inflammation and tissue damage. This manifests as:
    *   **Atypical Hemolytic Uremic Syndrome (aHUS):** Characterized by microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury. This is the most common severe manifestation.
    *   **Dense Deposit Disease (DDD) / Membranoproliferative Glomerulonephritis (MPGN) Type II:** C3 deposition in the glomeruli leads to kidney damage.
    *   **Recurrent Infections:** Impaired clearance of pathogens due to dysregulated complement activity.
    *   **Age-Related Macular Degeneration (AMD):** Complement activation contributes to retinal damage.
*   **Laboratory Findings in Factor H Deficiency:** C3 and C4 levels are typically normal or elevated because the primary defect is in regulation, not initiation or consumption (unless severe tissue damage occurs). Factor H activity is decreased.
*   **Connecting to the Case:** The patient's symptoms (recurrent UTIs, MPGN), family history, and lab findings (elevated C3/C4, decreased Factor H) are classic for Factor H deficiency leading to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to C5b-9 deficiency.** This would lead to increased susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections (e.g., meningitis, gonorrhea) and impaired bacterial lysis. It would *not* cause elevated C3/C4 or MPGN. Patients with C5b-9 deficiency typically have normal C3/C4 levels. This is incorrect.
- (B) **Impaired opsonization by C3b due to deficiency in C3 convertase.** Deficiency in C3 convertase (e.g., C3 nephritic factor in C3 glomerulopathy) leads to *increased* consumption of C3, resulting in *low* C3 levels. While it can cause glomerulonephritis, the C3 levels would be low, not elevated as seen in this patient. This is incorrect.
- (D) **Inability to initiate the classical complement pathway due to deficiency in C1q.** C1q deficiency impairs the classical pathway, leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*) and impaired immune complex clearance, potentially causing SLE-like symptoms. C3 and C4 levels would be low due to consumption. This is incorrect.
- (E) **Failure to activate the lectin pathway due to mannose-binding lectin (MBL) deficiency.** MBL deficiency impairs the lectin pathway, leading to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria and fungi. C3 and C4 levels are usually normal. While it can contribute to infections, it does not typically cause MPGN or the specific lab findings (elevated C3/C4, low Factor H) seen here. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Factor H Deficiency (Correct) | C5b-9 Deficiency (A) | C3 Convertase Deficiency (B) | C1q Deficiency (D) | MBL Deficiency (E) |
| :---------------------- | :--------------------------- | :------------------- | :--------------------------- | :----------------- | :----------------- |
| **Pathway Affected**     | Alternative (Regulation)     | Terminal (MAC)      | Alternative (Initiation)     | Classical (Initiation) | Lectin (Initiation) |
| **C3 Level**            | Normal/Elevated              | Normal              | Low                          | Low                | Normal             |
| **C4 Level**            | Normal/Elevated              | Normal              | Normal                       | Low                | Normal             |
| **Factor H Activity**   | Decreased                    | Normal              | Normal                       | Normal             | Normal             |
| **Primary Manifestation** | aHUS, MPGN, Infections       | *Neisseria* infections | C3G, MPGN                    | Encapsulated bacteria, SLE | Encapsulated bacteria, fungi |
| **Regulation Defect**   | Yes                          | No                  | No                           | No                 | No                 |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Factor H deficiency leads to uncontrolled alternative complement pathway activation, causing tissue damage (e.g., MPGN, aHUS) and potentially recurrent infections. Key lab findings include normal/elevated C3/C4 and decreased Factor H activity. Differentiate this from deficiencies in complement initiation (C1q, MBL) or terminal pathway (C5b-9), which present with different infection susceptibilities and complement level profiles (typically low C3/C4 for initiation defects). Also, distinguish from C3 convertase dysregulation (e.g., C3 nephritic factor) which causes low C3.

<a id='question-145'></a>

### Question 145: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 45 (Compartmentalization)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement regulatory proteins (specifically Factor H) in preventing complement-mediated damage to host cells, particularly in the context of immune complex deposition and autoimmune diseases like atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS). (理解补体调节蛋白（特别是Factor H）在防止补体介导的宿主细胞损伤中的作用，特别是在免疫复合物沉积和自身免疫性疾病（如非典型溶血性尿毒症综合征aHUS）的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of bloody diarrhea, abdominal pain, and decreased urine output. His mother reports he had a recent upper respiratory infection. Physical examination reveals pallor, petechiae, and mild edema. Laboratory studies show severe microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA) with schistocytes on peripheral blood smear, thrombocytopenia, and acute kidney injury (elevated creatinine and BUN). Serum complement levels (C3 and C4) are normal. A renal biopsy shows thrombotic microangiopathy with fibrin deposition in arterioles, but no significant immune complex deposition by immunofluorescence. Genetic testing reveals a homozygous mutation in the *CFH* gene.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's condition?

(A) Antibody-mediated activation of the classical complement pathway leading to C3 convertase formation on endothelial cells.
(B) Defective regulation of the alternative complement pathway on host cell surfaces, leading to excessive C3b deposition and amplification loop activation.
(C) Formation of immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli, activating the classical complement pathway and causing endothelial damage.
(D) Deficiency in C1 inhibitor leading to uncontrolled activation of the classical and alternative complement pathways.
(E) Genetic deficiency in Factor I, impairing the inactivation of C3 convertase and leading to chronic complement activation.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with bloody diarrhea (often precedes aHUS), abdominal pain, decreased urine output (AKI), pallor, petechiae (thrombocytopenia), edema (AKI). This constellation points towards thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA).
- **Laboratory Findings:** MAHA (schistocytes), thrombocytopenia, AKI. These are the classic triad of TMA.
- **Complement Levels:** Normal C3 and C4. This rules out conditions involving widespread consumption of complement components, such as classical pathway activation (which would consume C1, C4, C2) or severe alternative pathway activation (which would consume C3).
- **Renal Biopsy:** Thrombotic microangiopathy with fibrin deposition, but *no significant immune complex deposition*. This is crucial; it differentiates this TMA from immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis.
- **Genetic Testing:** Homozygous mutation in the *CFH* gene. *CFH* encodes Factor H, a key regulator of the alternative complement pathway.
- **Diagnosis:** The clinical picture (TMA), normal complement levels, lack of immune complex deposition, and *CFH* mutation strongly suggest atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS) caused by Factor H deficiency or dysfunction.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the condition.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of protein activation leading to opsonization, inflammation, and cell lysis. There are three pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), alternative (spontaneous activation), and lectin (mannose-binding lectin-dependent). All converge on C3 convertase (C3bBb for alternative/lectin, C4b2a for classical) and C5 convertase.
- **Alternative Pathway:** This pathway is constantly active at a low level ("tick-over") on host cell surfaces. C3 spontaneously hydrolyzes to C3(H2O), which binds Factor B. Factor D cleaves Factor B into Ba and Bb, forming the C3 convertase C3bBb. Factor I, in conjunction with cofactor proteins like Factor H and MCP (CD46), cleaves and inactivates C3bBb.
- **Factor H:** Factor H is the primary soluble regulator of the alternative pathway. It binds to C3b deposited on host cell surfaces, accelerating the dissociation of Bb from C3bBb, and acts as a cofactor for Factor I in cleaving C3b. It also competes with Factor B for binding to C3b. Factor H deficiency or dysfunction leads to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation on endothelial surfaces.
- **aHUS Pathogenesis:** In aHUS, dysregulation of the alternative pathway leads to excessive C3b deposition on endothelial cells. This recruits Factor B, forming C3 convertase (C3bBb), which cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b, amplifying the cascade. C3b opsonizes platelets and endothelial cells, leading to platelet aggregation, endothelial damage, inflammation, and thrombosis in small vessels (TMA). C5 convertase formation leads to C5a generation (inflammation, neutrophil recruitment) and MAC formation (cell lysis), contributing to endothelial injury.
- **Role of *CFH* Mutation:** Mutations in *CFH* (encoding Factor H) are the most common cause of inherited aHUS. These mutations impair Factor H's ability to regulate the alternative pathway, leading to excessive C3b deposition and amplification loop activation on endothelial surfaces.
- **Normal Complement Levels in aHUS:** Despite significant local complement activation on endothelial cells, systemic complement levels (C3, C4) are often normal in aHUS because the activation is primarily localized and self-limiting, or because the regulation defects are specific to certain cell types or locations.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated activation of the classical complement pathway. This would typically involve immune complexes or antibodies binding to cell surfaces, leading to consumption of C1, C4, and C2, resulting in *low* C4 levels. The vignette states normal C4 and no significant immune complex deposition. This mechanism is characteristic of classical pathway-mediated diseases like SLE nephritis or post-infectious glomerulonephritis, not this presentation.
- (C): Formation of immune complexes that deposit in the glomeruli, activating the classical complement pathway. Similar to (A), this involves immune complexes and classical pathway activation, leading to low C4 and significant immune complex deposition on biopsy, both of which are absent here. This describes immune complex-mediated glomerulonephritis.
- (D): Deficiency in C1 inhibitor (C1-INH). C1-INH deficiency causes hereditary angioedema (HAE) due to uncontrolled activation of the classical pathway (leading to bradykinin production) and the contact system. It can also cause consumption of C4 and C2. While complement activation occurs, the primary clinical manifestation is angioedema, not TMA, and C4 would typically be low.
- (E): Genetic deficiency in Factor I. Factor I is essential for inactivating both C3 convertases (C3bBb and C4b2a). Deficiency leads to impaired regulation of *both* the alternative and classical pathways, resulting in severe consumption of C3 and C4, leading to *low* C3 and C4 levels. This is not consistent with the normal complement levels observed in the patient.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (B) - aHUS due to Factor H deficiency | (A) Classical Pathway Activation (e.g., SLE) | (C) Immune Complex Glomerulonephritis | (D) C1-INH Deficiency (HAE) | (E) Factor I Deficiency |
|--------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Primary Pathway**      | Alternative                                          | Classical                                 | Classical                            | Classical/Contact System   | Alternative & Classical |
| **Complement Levels**   | Normal C3, C4                                        | Low C4, C2 (often low C3)                  | Low C4, C2 (often low C3)            | Low C4, C2                  | Low C3, C4            |
| **Immune Complexes**     | Absent/Minimal                                       | Present                                   | Present                              | Absent                      | Absent                |
| **Key Regulator Defect** | Factor H (CFH gene)                                  | None specific (autoantibodies common)     | None specific (immune complex formation) | C1 Inhibitor                | Factor I               |
| **Clinical Manifestation**| TMA (MAHA, Thrombocytopenia, AKI)                   | Glomerulonephritis, Vasculitis             | Glomerulonephritis                   | Angioedema                  | Severe Complement Deficiency |
| **Genetic Basis**        | Common (CFH, CFI, MCP, C3 mutations)                 | Less common                               | N/A                                  | Common (SERPING1 gene)      | Rare                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Atypical HUS (aHUS) is a life-threatening TMA often caused by genetic defects in complement regulatory proteins, most commonly Factor H (*CFH* gene). Key diagnostic features include TMA, normal complement levels (C3, C4), and absence of significant immune complex deposition. Differentiating aHUS from other causes of TMA (like Shiga toxin-producing E. coli infection, which typically causes diarrhea followed by HUS with *low* C3) and other complement disorders requires understanding the specific pathways and regulatory proteins involved. Remember that Factor H regulates the alternative pathway, and its deficiency leads to uncontrolled C3b deposition on endothelial surfaces.

<a id='question-146'></a>

### Question 146: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 46 (Figure)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement activation (specifically C5a) in recruiting neutrophils and its interaction with complement receptors (CR1, CR3) on neutrophils, and how deficiencies or dysregulation can lead to recurrent infections. (理解补体激活（特别是C5a）在募集中性粒细胞中的作用，以及其与中性粒细胞上的补体受体（CR1、CR3）的相互作用，以及缺陷或失调如何导致复发性感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with a 2-day history of fever, irritability, and difficulty breathing. His mother reports that he has had recurrent, severe bacterial infections since infancy, including pneumonia, otitis media, and skin abscesses. Physical examination reveals tachypnea, tachycardia, and diffuse rhonchi on lung auscultation. Laboratory studies show leukocytosis with a neutrophilia (95% neutrophils) and elevated C-reactive protein. A chest X-ray confirms bilateral pneumonia. Sputum Gram stain shows numerous Gram-negative diplococci. Blood cultures are pending. The patient's family history is significant for a maternal uncle who died of sepsis at age 5. Flow cytometry analysis of the patient's neutrophils reveals markedly reduced expression of CD18 (integrin β2 subunit).

(A) Deficiency in the alternative pathway of complement activation leading to impaired opsonization by C3b.
(B) Deficiency in the classical pathway of complement activation leading to impaired formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC).
(C) Deficiency in the C5 convertase leading to impaired cleavage of C5 and reduced C5a production.
(D) Deficiency in the formation of the NADPH oxidase complex leading to impaired intracellular killing of phagocytosed bacteria.
(E) Deficiency in the expression of CD18 leading to impaired neutrophil adhesion and migration to sites of infection.

#### Correct Answer: E

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with recurrent, severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, otitis media, skin abscesses) since infancy. This points towards an underlying immunodeficiency.
- **Current Illness:** Fever, irritability, difficulty breathing, tachypnea, tachycardia, rhonchi, bilateral pneumonia confirmed by chest X-ray. This indicates an acute bacterial infection, likely pneumonia.
- **Microbiology:** Sputum Gram stain shows Gram-negative diplococci, suggestive of *Neisseria* species (e.g., *N. meningitidis*, *N. gonorrhoeae*) or *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. The recurrent nature and severity suggest a defect in host defense.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Leukocytosis with neutrophilia (95% neutrophils) indicates the bone marrow is producing neutrophils, but they are likely not functioning properly or reaching the site of infection effectively. Elevated CRP confirms inflammation/infection.
- **Family History:** Maternal uncle died of sepsis at age 5, suggesting a possible inherited immunodeficiency.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Markedly reduced expression of CD18 on neutrophils. CD18 is the β2 subunit of several integrins, including LFA-1 (CD11a/CD18) and Mac-1 (CD11b/CD18). These integrins are crucial for neutrophil adhesion to endothelial cells and subsequent migration into tissues.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **CD18 Deficiency (Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency Type 1, LAD-1):** CD18 is essential for the formation of β2 integrins (LFA-1, Mac-1, p150,95). These integrins mediate the firm adhesion of leukocytes (especially neutrophils) to endothelial cells expressing ICAM-1 (Intercellular Adhesion Molecule-1) and other ligands. This adhesion step is critical for extravasation (diapedesis) from the bloodstream into infected tissues.
- **Mechanism:** Without functional CD18, neutrophils cannot adhere firmly to the endothelium, preventing their migration to sites of infection. This leads to a marked increase in circulating neutrophils (neutrophilia) because they cannot leave the bloodstream, but a severe lack of neutrophils at the site of infection, resulting in recurrent bacterial infections, particularly involving skin, mucous membranes, and lungs. The infections are often caused by catalase-positive organisms (like *Staphylococcus aureus*, *Serratia marcescens*, *Burkholderia cepacia*, *Aspergillus*) because neutrophils are needed to generate reactive oxygen species (ROS) to kill these organisms. Impaired wound healing is also characteristic due to lack of neutrophil influx.
- **Complement Connection:** While complement activation (especially C5a) is a chemoattractant for neutrophils, the *primary* defect in LAD-1 is the inability of neutrophils to *adhere* and *migrate*, not necessarily a defect in complement activation itself or the response to C5a. However, the lack of neutrophils at the site of infection indirectly impacts the inflammatory response, including complement activation. The question specifically asks about the *underlying defect* based on the CD18 finding.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Deficiency in the alternative pathway of complement activation leading to impaired opsonization by C3b. This would cause recurrent infections, especially with encapsulated bacteria (like *Neisseria meningitidis*, *Streptococcus pneumoniae*, *Haemophilus influenzae*). However, the key finding here is the CD18 deficiency, which directly points to a neutrophil adhesion/migration defect, not a primary complement pathway defect. While complement opsonization is important, the inability of neutrophils to reach the site of infection is the central problem in LAD-1.
- (B) Deficiency in the classical pathway of complement activation leading to impaired formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC). Deficiencies in the terminal complement components (C5-C9) impair MAC formation, increasing susceptibility to *Neisseria* infections. However, this doesn't explain the CD18 deficiency or the neutrophilia.
- (C) Deficiency in the C5 convertase leading to impaired cleavage of C5 and reduced C5a production. C5a is a potent chemoattractant for neutrophils. A deficiency would impair neutrophil recruitment. However, the primary defect identified is CD18 deficiency, which affects adhesion *after* chemotaxis has occurred. Furthermore, while C5a is important, neutrophils still need to adhere and migrate.
- (D) Deficiency in the formation of the NADPH oxidase complex leading to impaired intracellular killing of phagocytosed bacteria. This describes Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD). Patients with CGD have recurrent infections with catalase-positive organisms, similar to LAD-1. However, neutrophils in CGD can migrate to the site of infection; their defect is in intracellular killing *after* phagocytosis. The flow cytometry finding of CD18 deficiency specifically rules out CGD and points to LAD-1.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | LAD-1 (CD18 Deficiency) | CGD (NADPH Oxidase Defect) | Complement Deficiency (e.g., C3, C5-C9) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :-------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Neutrophil Adhesion/Migration | Intracellular Killing      | Complement Activation/Function         |
| **Key Molecule**        | CD18 (β2 integrin subunit) | NADPH Oxidase Components   | C3, C5, C6, C7, C8, C9, Factor B, Factor D, Properdin |
| **Cell Type Affected**  | Neutrophils             | Neutrophils, Macrophages   | All cells involved in complement cascade |
| **Characteristic Finding** | Neutrophilia, Delayed Umbilical Cord Separation, Impaired Wound Healing | Granuloma Formation, Catalase-Positive Infections | Recurrent Encapsulated Bacterial Infections (C3), Neisseria Infections (C5-C9) |
| **Flow Cytometry**      | Reduced CD18 expression | Normal CD18 expression     | Normal CD18 expression                 |
| **Typical Infections**  | Skin, Mucous Membranes, Lungs | Skin, Lungs, Lymph Nodes, Liver | Encapsulated Bacteria, Neisseria        |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency Type 1 (LAD-1) is caused by defects in the β2 integrin subunit (CD18), leading to impaired neutrophil adhesion and migration. This results in recurrent bacterial infections without pus formation, neutrophilia, and delayed umbilical cord separation. Differentiate LAD-1 from Chronic Granulomatous Disease (CGD) by recognizing that CGD involves impaired intracellular killing, while LAD-1 involves impaired migration. Complement deficiencies typically present with infections by specific types of encapsulated bacteria or *Neisseria*.

<a id='question-147'></a>

### Question 147: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 47 (Compartmentalization)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of complement regulatory proteins (specifically Factor H) in preventing complement-mediated damage to host cells, particularly in the context of immune complex deposition and autoimmune diseases like atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS). (理解补体调节蛋白（特别是Factor H）在防止补体介导的宿主细胞损伤中的作用，特别是在免疫复合物沉积和自身免疫性疾病（如非典型溶血性尿毒症综合征aHUS）的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 3-year-old boy presents to the emergency department with acute onset of bloody diarrhea, pallor, and oliguria. His mother reports he had a recent upper respiratory infection treated with amoxicillin. Physical examination reveals petechiae, jaundice, and mild hypertension. Laboratory studies show severe microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA) with schistocytes on peripheral blood smear, thrombocytopenia, and elevated serum creatinine and BUN. A direct antiglobulin test (DAT, Coombs test) is negative. Serum complement levels (C3 and C4) are normal. Urinalysis shows proteinuria and hematuria. Renal biopsy reveals glomerular endothelial cell swelling and detachment, along with deposition of C3 and Factor B fragments in the glomerular capillaries, but no significant immune complex deposition (IgG or IgM). Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *CFH* gene, leading to reduced levels of circulating Factor H.

Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for the patient's condition?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to classical pathway C3 convertase formation on host cells.
(B) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to a deficiency in C5b-9 assembly.
(C) Unregulated complement activation on endothelial surfaces due to insufficient inhibition of the alternative pathway C3 convertase.
(D) Formation of immune complexes that activate the lectin pathway, leading to C3b deposition and opsonization of erythrocytes.
(E) Defective phagocytosis of immune complexes by macrophages due to impaired Fc receptor function.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 3-year-old boy with bloody diarrhea, pallor, oliguria, petechiae, jaundice, hypertension. This constellation suggests microangiopathic hemolytic anemia (MAHA) and acute kidney injury (AKI).
- **Laboratory Findings:** MAHA (schistocytes), thrombocytopenia, elevated creatinine/BUN (AKI). These are hallmarks of thrombotic microangiopathy (TMA).
- **Negative DAT:** Rules out autoimmune hemolytic anemia mediated by antibodies against red blood cells.
- **Normal C3/C4:** Suggests the complement system is not globally consumed, pointing towards localized or dysregulated activation rather than systemic activation like in some immune complex diseases.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Glomerular endothelial cell swelling/detachment, C3/Factor B deposition in capillaries, *no significant immune complex deposition*. This indicates complement activation is occurring directly on the endothelial surface, not primarily driven by antibody deposition.
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous *CFH* mutation leading to reduced Factor H levels. Factor H is a key regulator of the *alternative pathway* of complement activation.
- **Connecting the Clues:** The clinical picture is TMA (specifically, likely atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome, aHUS). The renal biopsy shows complement activation on endothelial cells without significant immune complexes. The genetic defect is in Factor H, a regulator of the alternative pathway. This strongly points to dysregulation of the alternative pathway leading to complement-mediated endothelial damage.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Complement System Overview:** The complement system is a crucial part of innate immunity, involving a cascade of proteins leading to opsonization, inflammation, and cell lysis. There are three activation pathways: classical (antibody-dependent), lectin (mannose-binding lectin-dependent), and alternative (spontaneous activation on surfaces).
- **Alternative Pathway:** Initiated by spontaneous hydrolysis of C3 to C3(H2O) or by C3b deposition on surfaces. C3(H2O) binds Factor B, which is cleaved by Factor D to form the fluid-phase C3 convertase (C3bBb). On cell surfaces, C3bBb forms the surface-bound C3 convertase. This convertase cleaves C3 into C3a and C3b, amplifying the cascade.
- **Regulation of Alternative Pathway:** Crucial to prevent complement activation on host cells. Key regulators include:
    - **Factor H:** Binds C3b and acts as a cofactor for Factor I, which cleaves C3b into inactive iC3b. It also competes with Factor B for binding to C3b and accelerates the decay of the C3 convertase (C3bBb). Deficiency or dysfunction of Factor H leads to uncontrolled alternative pathway activation.
    - **Factor I:** Serine protease that cleaves C3b and C4b.
    - **CD59 (Protectin):** Inhibits the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) by preventing the binding of C9.
    - **Decay-Accelerating Factor (DAF/CD55):** Accelerates the decay of C3 convertases (C3bBb and C4b2a).
- **aHUS:** A rare genetic disorder characterized by TMA (MAHA, thrombocytopenia, AKI). Most cases are caused by mutations in complement regulatory genes, particularly *CFH* (Factor H), *CFI* (Factor I), *CD46* (membrane cofactor protein, MCP), *C3* (gain-of-function mutations), or *CFB* (Factor B). The underlying mechanism is uncontrolled activation of the alternative pathway on endothelial surfaces, leading to endothelial damage, platelet activation, microthrombi formation, and consumption of platelets and red blood cells.
- **Patient's Case:** The patient has a *CFH* mutation leading to reduced Factor H. This impairs the regulation of the alternative pathway C3 convertase (C3bBb) on endothelial surfaces. Uncontrolled C3b deposition leads to amplification of the cascade, formation of C5 convertase (C3bBbC3b), cleavage of C5, and formation of the MAC (C5b-9). The MAC inserts into endothelial cell membranes, causing cell damage and lysis, triggering TMA. The deposition of C3 and Factor B fragments (part of the C3 convertase) on the glomeruli confirms localized complement activation. The absence of significant immune complexes rules out classical or lectin pathway activation driven by antibodies or mannose-binding lectin.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to classical pathway C3 convertase formation on host cells. This describes classical pathway activation, typically involving IgG or IgM binding to antigens. The patient has a negative DAT and no significant immune complex deposition on renal biopsy, making this unlikely. Classical pathway activation usually involves C1q, C4, and C2, leading to C4b2a (C3 convertase).
- (B) Defective formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC) due to a deficiency in C5b-9 assembly. This would lead to increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria (e.g., *Neisseria*) but would not cause the complement-mediated endothelial damage seen in aHUS. The patient's condition is characterized by *excessive* MAC formation on endothelial cells due to uncontrolled upstream activation.
- (D) Formation of immune complexes that activate the lectin pathway, leading to C3b deposition and opsonization of erythrocytes. This describes lectin pathway activation, initiated by mannose-binding lectin binding to microbial surfaces or certain host cell glycans. While immune complexes can activate the classical pathway, the vignette specifically states *no significant immune complex deposition*. Lectin pathway activation is less commonly associated with aHUS.
- (E) Defective phagocytosis of immune complexes by macrophages due to impaired Fc receptor function. This would lead to accumulation of immune complexes, potentially causing systemic lupus erythematosus (SLE) or other immune complex diseases, but it does not directly explain the localized complement activation on endothelial cells and the specific genetic defect in Factor H. Phagocytosis defects are related to C3b opsonization and Fc receptor function, not the primary regulation of complement activation pathways.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | aHUS (Patient's Case)                                  | Classical Pathway Activation (e.g., SLE nephritis) | Lectin Pathway Activation (e.g., certain infections) | Alternative Pathway Deficiency (e.g., Factor I deficiency) | MAC Deficiency (e.g., C5-C9 deficiency) |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Defect**      | Factor H deficiency (or other complement regulator)   | Immune complex deposition (IgG/IgM)                 | MBL binding to pathogen/host surface                  | Deficiency in Factor I, Factor H, CD46, etc.               | Deficiency in C5, C6, C7, C8, C9         |
| **Complement Activation** | Unregulated Alternative Pathway on host endothelium   | Classical Pathway (C1q, C4, C2)                   | Lectin Pathway (MBL, MASPs)                          | Unregulated Alternative Pathway (due to lack of regulation) | Normal activation, impaired terminal steps |
| **Immune Complexes**    | Absent or minimal                                     | Present                                            | Usually absent                                       | Absent or minimal                                          | Absent or minimal                        |
| **C3/C4 Levels**        | Normal (localized activation)                          | Often low (systemic consumption)                   | Normal (localized activation)                        | Normal (localized activation)                             | Normal                                   |
| **Clinical Manifestation** | TMA (MAHA, thrombocytopenia, AKI)                    | Glomerulonephritis, vasculitis, etc.               | Infections, inflammation                             | Increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria, TMA      | Increased susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria |
| **Key Regulator**       | Factor H (deficient in this case)                     | C1 inhibitor                                       | MBL-associated serine proteases (MASPs)              | Factor H, Factor I, CD55, CD59                             | None directly involved in activation     |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Atypical hemolytic uremic syndrome (aHUS) is often caused by genetic defects in complement regulatory proteins, most commonly Factor H (*CFH* gene), leading to uncontrolled activation of the alternative complement pathway on endothelial surfaces. This results in endothelial damage, microthrombi formation, and thrombotic microangiopathy. Key features distinguishing aHUS from typical HUS (Shiga toxin-mediated) include normal C3/C4 levels, negative DAT, and absence of significant immune complex deposition. Differentiating aHUS from other complement disorders requires understanding the specific pathways and regulatory mechanisms involved.

<a id='question-148'></a>

### Question 148: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 48 (Dendritic)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Dendritic cells (DCs) are professional antigen-presenting cells (APCs) crucial for initiating adaptive immunity. They capture antigens in peripheral tissues, migrate to lymph nodes, mature, and present processed antigens via MHC class I and II molecules to T cells, along with co-stimulatory signals (e.g., B7/CD80/CD86) and cytokines, thereby bridging innate and adaptive immunity. Different DC subsets exist with specialized functions. (树突状细胞 (DCs) 是专业的抗原提呈细胞 (APCs)，对于启动适应性免疫至关重要。它们在周围组织中捕获抗原，迁移到淋巴结，成熟，并通过 MHC I 类和 II 类分子向 T 细胞提呈加工后的抗原，同时提供共刺激信号（例如 B7/CD80/CD86）和细胞因子，从而连接先天性和适应性免疫。存在具有专门功能的不同 DC 子集。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) undergoes elective splenectomy following traumatic rupture. Three weeks post-surgery, he develops fever, chills, and a productive cough with purulent sputum. Chest X-ray reveals a right lower lobe consolidation. Sputum Gram stain shows numerous Gram-positive cocci in pairs (diplococci). Culture of the sputum grows *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. The patient is treated with intravenous ceftriaxone, but his condition worsens, and he develops septic shock. Blood cultures become positive for *S. pneumoniae*. Further investigation reveals that the patient's spleen, although removed, was previously involved in CLL, leading to impaired splenic function even before the splenectomy. The patient's physician suspects an underlying immune defect contributing to the severe infection. Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood reveals a markedly reduced number of CD11c+CD14- cells compared to healthy controls.

Which of the following cell types is most likely deficient in this patient, contributing to his susceptibility to overwhelming encapsulated bacterial infection?

(A) Neutrophils
(B) Natural Killer (NK) cells
(C) Monocytes
(D) Dendritic cells
(E) B cells

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with CLL, post-splenectomy. CLL is associated with immune dysfunction, including defects in B-cell function and often affecting other immune cells. Splenectomy increases susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria like *S. pneumoniae* due to the spleen's role in filtering these organisms from the blood.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, chills, productive cough, purulent sputum, right lower lobe consolidation – classic signs of bacterial pneumonia, specifically pneumococcal pneumonia.
- **Microbiological Findings:** Sputum Gram stain (Gram-positive diplococci) and culture (*Streptococcus pneumoniae*) confirm the causative agent.
- **Clinical Course:** Worsening condition despite appropriate antibiotic therapy (ceftriaxone), leading to septic shock and positive blood cultures, indicating overwhelming infection. This suggests a significant underlying immune defect.
- **Immunological Clue:** Flow cytometry shows reduced CD11c+CD14- cells. CD11c is a marker expressed by various myeloid cells, including DCs, monocytes, macrophages, and some B cells. CD14 is a marker primarily expressed by monocytes and macrophages. Therefore, CD11c+CD14- cells are likely identifying a subset of DCs (specifically, conventional DCs or plasmacytoid DCs, depending on the specific antibody panel used, but the key is the reduction in a DC population).
- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has CLL, is post-splenectomy, and develops severe, overwhelming pneumococcal sepsis despite antibiotics. Flow cytometry shows a deficiency in CD11c+CD14- cells. This points towards a defect in dendritic cells, which are crucial for initiating T-cell responses against encapsulated bacteria. The spleen is also a major site for DC function. The combination of CLL, splenectomy, and reduced DCs strongly suggests a compromised ability to mount an effective adaptive immune response against *S. pneumoniae*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Dendritic Cells (DCs):** DCs are professional APCs that capture antigens in tissues, process them, and migrate to lymph nodes to present them to T cells. They express MHC class I and II molecules, co-stimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86, also known as B7), and secrete cytokines (like IL-12) that direct T-cell differentiation. Different DC subsets exist (e.g., conventional DCs type 1 and 2, plasmacytoid DCs).
- **Role in *S. pneumoniae* Infection:** DCs play a critical role in initiating the adaptive immune response against *S. pneumoniae*. They phagocytose the bacteria, process capsular polysaccharide and protein antigens, and present them to T cells. IL-12 produced by DCs promotes Th1 responses, crucial for opsonization and clearance of encapsulated bacteria.
- **CLL and Immune Dysfunction:** CLL is characterized by the accumulation of malignant B cells, leading to suppression of normal immune function. This includes defects in T-cell function, NK cell activity, and often abnormalities in myeloid cells, including DCs and monocytes/macrophages.
- **Splenectomy and *S. pneumoniae*:** The spleen filters blood and is rich in macrophages and DCs, playing a vital role in clearing encapsulated bacteria like *S. pneumoniae*. Splenectomy significantly increases the risk of overwhelming post-splenectomy infection (OPSI) by these organisms.
- **CD11c and CD14 Markers:** CD11c (integrin alpha X) is expressed on various myeloid cells, including DCs, monocytes, macrophages, NK cells, and some B cells. CD14 is a lipopolysaccharide (LPS) receptor primarily expressed on monocytes and macrophages. The combination CD11c+CD14- is commonly used to identify a significant population of DCs in peripheral blood, particularly conventional DCs (cDCs). A reduction in this population would impair antigen presentation and T-cell activation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Neutrophils: Neutrophils are phagocytes crucial for the *innate* response against bacteria. While important, the patient's severe sepsis despite antibiotics and the specific flow cytometry finding point more towards a defect in *adaptive* immunity initiation (antigen presentation), rather than phagocytosis itself. Neutrophils are typically CD11b+ (a marker similar to CD11c but more specific for neutrophils) and CD14 low/negative. A deficiency would impair initial bacterial killing but doesn't directly explain the flow cytometry result or the failure to mount an effective adaptive response.
- (B) Natural Killer (NK) cells: NK cells are part of the innate immune system, primarily involved in killing virus-infected cells and tumor cells. They express CD16 and CD56 but typically lack CD11c and CD14. While NK cell function can be impaired in CLL, they are not the primary cells responsible for initiating T-cell responses against bacteria, nor do they fit the CD11c+CD14- phenotype.
- (C) Monocytes: Monocytes are precursors to macrophages and DCs. They are phagocytic and also function as APCs. They express CD14 and CD11c. While monocyte/macrophage function can be affected in CLL, the flow cytometry specifically identifies CD11c+CD14-* cells, which are typically considered DCs, distinct from the monocyte population (CD11c+CD14+). A primary monocyte deficiency would be less likely given the specific marker profile provided.
- (E) B cells: B cells are responsible for humoral immunity (antibody production). While CLL involves malignant B cells and can impair normal B-cell function, the primary defect leading to susceptibility to encapsulated bacteria is often impaired T-cell help (due to defective APCs like DCs) or impaired antibody production itself. However, the flow cytometry finding (CD11c+CD14-) does not primarily identify B cells (which are typically CD19+ or CD20+). While some B cells can express CD11c, they are generally CD14 negative, but the CD11c+CD14- population is classically associated with DCs. The core problem in overwhelming infection by encapsulated bacteria is often defective T-cell priming by APCs.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Dendritic Cells (DCs) | Neutrophils        | NK Cells          | Monocytes          | B Cells            |
|----------------------|-----------------------|--------------------|-------------------|--------------------|--------------------|
| **Primary Role**     | Antigen Presentation  | Phagocytosis       | Cytotoxicity      | Phagocytosis, APC  | Antibody Production|
| **Key Markers**      | CD11c, MHC II, B7     | CD11b, CD16, CD66b | CD16, CD56        | CD14, CD11c        | CD19, CD20, BCR    |
| **Flow Cytometry ID**| CD11c+CD14- (subset) | CD11b+CD14 low/-   | CD16+CD56+        | CD11c+CD14+        | CD19+CD20+         |
| **Role in *S. pneumo***| T-cell priming       | Initial killing    | Limited           | Phagocytosis, APC  | Antibody production|
| **Deficiency Effect**| Impaired T-cell help | Impaired killing   | Impaired killing  | Impaired phagocytosis/APC | Impaired Ab response|
| **Relevance to Case**| Matches flow data, links to adaptive failure | Less likely given flow data | Less likely given flow data | Doesn't fit flow data | Affected by CLL, but flow data points elsewhere |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Dendritic cells are essential APCs bridging innate and adaptive immunity, particularly crucial for initiating T-cell responses against pathogens like *Streptococcus pneumoniae*. Defects in DC number or function, as seen in certain immunodeficiencies or conditions like CLL, can lead to severe susceptibility to encapsulated bacterial infections. Flow cytometry using markers like CD11c and CD14 helps identify and quantify DC populations. Differential diagnosis for overwhelming encapsulated bacterial infections includes splenectomy, complement deficiencies, B-cell defects (e.g., XLA, agammaglobulinemia), and T-cell defects. The specific flow cytometry finding in this case points directly to a DC deficiency.

<a id='question-149'></a>

### Question 149: Graft-versus-Host Disease (GVHD) Pathogenesis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of the cellular and molecular mechanisms underlying acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD), specifically the roles of donor T cells, recipient antigen-presenting cells (APCs), and the resulting inflammatory cascade in the target organs. (考察移植后GVHD的细胞和分子机制，特别是供者T细胞、受者抗原提呈细胞的作用以及靶器官的炎症级联反应。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with acute myeloid leukemia (AML) undergoes allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplantation (HSCT) from a matched unrelated donor. The conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and total body irradiation. Post-transplant, the patient develops fever, watery diarrhea, and a maculopapular rash starting on the trunk and spreading centrifugally. Liver function tests show elevated bilirubin and alkaline phosphatase. Skin biopsy reveals apoptotic keratinocytes and lymphocytic infiltrate. Flow cytometry on the skin biopsy shows a predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin. Further investigation reveals that the patient's recipient APCs express high levels of MHC class II molecules and costimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86) in response to the conditioning regimen-induced tissue damage. The patient's serum contains antibodies against donor HLA antigens.

Which of the following cellular interactions is MOST critical for initiating the inflammatory cascade observed in this patient's skin?

(A) Donor CD4+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class I molecules on keratinocytes.
(B) Donor CD8+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class I molecules presenting self-antigens on keratinocytes.
(C) Donor CD8+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class II molecules presenting donor-derived antigens on recipient APCs.
(D) Donor CD4+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class II molecules presenting donor-derived antigens on recipient APCs.
(E) Donor NK cells recognizing recipient cells lacking MHC class I molecules.

#### Correct Answer: (D)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male with AML undergoing allogeneic HSCT. This sets the stage for potential GVHD.
- **Conditioning Regimen:** Fludarabine and total body irradiation. This regimen causes significant tissue damage (especially to the gut, skin, and liver) and downregulates T cell function in the recipient, but also upregulates MHC and costimulatory molecules on recipient APCs.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Fever, watery diarrhea, maculopapular rash (classic triad of acute GVHD), elevated liver enzymes. These are hallmark signs of acute GVHD affecting the gut, skin, and liver.
- **Skin Biopsy:** Apoptotic keratinocytes and lymphocytic infiltrate. This confirms skin involvement and suggests an immune-mediated process.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Predominance of CD8+ T cells expressing granzyme B and perforin in the skin infiltrate. This indicates cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL)-mediated damage, characteristic of acute GVHD.
- **Recipient APCs:** High expression of MHC class II and costimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86). This is a consequence of tissue damage caused by the conditioning regimen, making them potent activators of donor T cells.
- **Serum Antibodies:** Antibodies against donor HLA antigens. While antibodies can play a role in chronic GVHD, the acute presentation described here is primarily T cell-mediated.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST critical cellular interaction initiating the inflammatory cascade in the skin. This focuses on the specific T cell recognition event driving the pathology.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **GVHD Pathogenesis:** Acute GVHD is primarily mediated by donor T cells recognizing recipient antigens as foreign. The conditioning regimen damages recipient tissues, leading to the release of damage-associated molecular patterns (DAMPs) and upregulation of MHC class II and costimulatory molecules (CD80/CD86) on recipient APCs (like dendritic cells, macrophages).
- **T Cell Activation:** Donor T cells, particularly CD4+ helper T cells and CD8+ cytotoxic T cells, migrate from the graft to the recipient's tissues.
- **Antigen Presentation:** Recipient APCs present processed antigens (both self-antigens and antigens derived from the graft, like donor-specific antigens) on MHC molecules to donor T cells.
- **Key Interaction:** The critical initiating event involves donor T cells recognizing these antigens presented by recipient APCs.
    - **CD4+ T cells:** Recognize antigens presented on MHC class II molecules. They play a crucial role in orchestrating the immune response, including activating CD8+ T cells and producing cytokines that contribute to inflammation. In GVHD, donor CD4+ T cells recognize recipient MHC class II molecules presenting processed antigens (including donor-derived antigens released from damaged graft cells or recipient self-antigens) on recipient APCs.
    - **CD8+ T cells:** Recognize antigens presented on MHC class I molecules. They are the primary effector cells causing direct cytotoxicity against recipient target cells (e.g., keratinocytes in the skin, hepatocytes in the liver, enterocytes in the gut). In GVHD, donor CD8+ T cells recognize recipient MHC class I molecules presenting processed antigens (often self-antigens, but also potentially donor-derived antigens) on recipient target cells or recipient APCs.
- **Role of APCs:** Recipient APCs are crucial because they present the antigens to the donor T cells. The upregulation of MHC class II and costimulatory molecules on these APCs following conditioning makes them highly effective at activating donor T cells.
- **Specific Scenario:** The vignette describes skin involvement with CD8+ T cells causing damage. While CD8+ T cells directly kill target cells, CD4+ T cells are essential for initiating and amplifying the response. The question asks for the *initiating* interaction for the *inflammatory cascade*. Donor CD4+ T cells recognizing recipient APCs presenting donor-derived antigens (released from damaged graft cells) or recipient self-antigens (presented in the context of inflammation) is a key step in activating the entire GVHD response, including the activation and recruitment of CD8+ T cells. The recipient APCs are presenting antigens (including donor-derived ones) on MHC class II molecules, which are recognized by donor CD4+ T cells.

##### 3. Why Option (D) is Correct:
- Donor CD4+ T cells are essential initiators of the adaptive immune response in GVHD.
- They recognize antigens presented on MHC class II molecules.
- Recipient APCs, upregulated by conditioning, present these antigens (including donor-derived antigens from damaged graft cells or recipient self-antigens) on MHC class II.
- This interaction activates donor CD4+ T cells, leading to cytokine production and activation of other immune cells, including CD8+ T cells, driving the inflammatory cascade and tissue damage characteristic of GVHD.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Donor CD4+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class I molecules on keratinocytes. CD4+ T cells recognize MHC class II, not MHC class I. This is incorrect.
- (B) Donor CD8+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class I molecules presenting self-antigens on keratinocytes. While CD8+ T cells are effector cells in GVHD skin damage and recognize MHC class I presenting antigens (often self-antigens), the *initiating* interaction involving APCs is typically driven by CD4+ T cells recognizing MHC class II. Furthermore, keratinocytes themselves are not professional APCs and express low levels of MHC class II under normal conditions (though this can be upregulated by inflammation). The primary interaction involves donor T cells recognizing antigens presented by recipient APCs. This option describes the effector mechanism but not the critical initiating interaction involving APCs.
- (C) Donor CD8+ T cells recognizing recipient MHC class II molecules presenting donor-derived antigens on recipient APCs. CD8+ T cells recognize MHC class I, not MHC class II. This is incorrect.
- (E) Donor NK cells recognizing recipient cells lacking MHC class I molecules. This describes a mechanism of NK cell-mediated lysis, which can contribute to GVHD (especially in the gut), but the primary driver of acute GVHD, particularly the systemic inflammatory response involving T cells and APCs, is T cell-mediated recognition of antigens presented by APCs. The vignette specifically highlights the role of T cells (CD8+ infiltrate) and the interaction with APCs (MHC II/costimulatory molecule upregulation). NK cells are part of the innate response and are less central to the specific adaptive T cell-APC interaction described.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature | Acute GVHD (Option D) | Chronic GVHD | NK Cell-mediated GVHD (Option E) | Autoimmune Disease |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Primary Mediators** | Donor CD4+ & CD8+ T cells | Donor T cells (CD4+, CD8+, Tregs), B cells, NK cells | Donor NK cells | Self-reactive T/B cells |
| **Key Antigen Recognition** | Donor T cells recognize recipient APCs (MHC II) presenting donor/self-antigens | Complex, involves T cell recognition of recipient/donor antigens | NK cells recognize recipient cells lacking MHC I | Self-antigens presented by self-APCs |
| **Timing** | Days to weeks post-HSCT | Months to years post-HSCT | Can occur early or late | Variable |
| **Target Organs** | Skin, liver, gut | Skin, liver, gut, lungs, eyes, etc. (more widespread) | Primarily gut | Variable, often multi-organ |
| **Histology** | Apoptotic cells, lymphocytic infiltrate | Fibrosis, atrophy, inflammation | Crypt abscesses, villous atrophy | Variable, inflammation, tissue damage |
| **Initiating Event** | Conditioning regimen -> APC activation -> T cell activation | Less clear, may involve alloantigen persistence, immune dysregulation | Often related to HLA mismatch, NK cell activation | Breakdown of self-tolerance |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Acute GVHD is primarily driven by donor T cells recognizing recipient antigens presented by recipient APCs. CD4+ T cells play a critical initiating role by recognizing antigens on MHC class II molecules presented by APCs, leading to activation of CD8+ cytotoxic T cells and the inflammatory cascade. Differentiate GVHD from other post-transplant complications like infection, rejection (recipient T cells attacking donor graft), or drug toxicity. Remember the key role of APCs in presenting antigens to donor T cells in the context of conditioning-induced tissue damage.

<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Innate Immune Response Meets Adaptive: Part 3 Focus 50 (Medulla)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific innate immune cells (e.g., macrophages, dendritic cells, NK cells) and their receptors (e.g., TLRs, PRRs) in initiating and shaping adaptive immune responses, particularly in the context of antigen presentation and T cell activation. (理解特异性先天免疫细胞（如巨噬细胞、树突状细胞、NK细胞）及其受体（如TLRs、PRRs）在启动和塑造适应性免疫反应中的作用，特别是在抗原呈递和T细胞激活的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 62-year-old male with a history of chronic myeloid leukemia (CML) treated with imatinib presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, and worsening shortness of breath. He recently underwent an elective splenectomy due to massive splenomegaly secondary to CML. Physical examination reveals tachypnea, tachycardia, and diffuse crackles on lung auscultation. Chest X-ray shows bilateral pulmonary infiltrates. Sputum Gram stain reveals numerous Gram-negative rods. Blood cultures are pending. The patient's white blood cell count is markedly elevated (150,000/µL) despite imatinib therapy, with a predominance of immature granulocytes (myelocytes, metamyelocytes). A bronchoalveolar lavage (BAL) fluid analysis shows a significant increase in neutrophils and macrophages. Flow cytometry analysis of BAL fluid macrophages reveals increased expression of CD80 and CD86 surface markers. Which of the following is the MOST likely mechanism by which these macrophages are contributing to the patient's immune response against the causative pathogen?

(A) Phagocytosis of opsonized bacteria followed by degradation within lysosomes.
(B) Presentation of pathogen-associated molecular patterns (PAMPs) to T helper cells via MHC class II molecules.
(C) Secretion of perforin and granzymes to induce apoptosis in infected host cells.
(D) Release of histamine and other inflammatory mediators to increase vascular permeability.
(E) Production of antibodies specific to the bacterial surface antigens.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 62-year-old male with CML (a myeloproliferative neoplasm characterized by excessive production of myeloid cells) treated with imatinib, status post splenectomy. This history suggests potential immunocompromise (CML itself, splenectomy removes a major site of antibody production and filtration of blood-borne pathogens) and a high risk for infection.
- **Presentation:** Acute onset fever, chills, shortness of breath, tachypnea, tachycardia, diffuse crackles, bilateral pulmonary infiltrates. This clinical picture is highly suggestive of pneumonia, likely bacterial given the acute onset and severity.
- **Microbiology:** Sputum Gram stain shows Gram-negative rods. This narrows down the potential pathogens (e.g., *Pseudomonas aeruginosa*, *Klebsiella pneumoniae*, *Escherichia coli*, *Acinetobacter baumannii*).
- **Hematology:** Markedly elevated WBC count (150,000/µL) with immature granulocytes despite imatinib. This indicates poor control of CML and potentially a blast crisis or accelerated phase, further compromising immunity.
- **Immunology/Pathology:** BAL fluid shows increased neutrophils and macrophages. Flow cytometry of BAL macrophages shows increased expression of CD80 and CD86. CD80 and CD86 are costimulatory molecules (also known as B7 molecules) upregulated on antigen-presenting cells (APCs), such as macrophages and dendritic cells, upon activation by PAMPs or DAMPs. Their upregulation is crucial for initiating T cell activation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism by which these activated macrophages are contributing to the immune response. The key is the upregulation of CD80/CD86, indicating antigen presentation function.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Innate Immune Activation:** The Gram-negative bacteria in the lungs are recognized by pattern recognition receptors (PRRs) on macrophages, such as Toll-like receptors (TLRs), particularly TLR4 recognizing lipopolysaccharide (LPS).
- **Macrophage Activation & Antigen Presentation:** Recognition of PAMPs (like LPS) by PRRs triggers macrophage activation. Activated macrophages mature into potent APCs. They phagocytose the bacteria, process bacterial antigens into peptides, and load these peptides onto MHC class II molecules.
- **Costimulation:** Crucially, PRR activation also leads to the upregulation of costimulatory molecules like CD80 (B7-1) and CD86 (B7-2) on the macrophage surface.
- **T Cell Activation:** These MHC class II-peptide complexes, along with the upregulated CD80/CD86 costimulatory molecules, are presented to CD4+ T helper cells (Th cells) in the lymph nodes or local tissues. The T cell receptor (TCR) on the Th cell must recognize the MHC II-peptide complex, and the CD28 receptor on the Th cell must bind to CD80/CD86 on the macrophage. This dual signaling (TCR-MHC + CD28-CD80/86) is essential for full T cell activation.
- **Adaptive Immune Response:** Activated Th cells then differentiate into various subsets (e.g., Th1, Th2, Th17) and orchestrate the adaptive immune response, including activating B cells to produce antibodies and enhancing macrophage killing activity.
- **Option Analysis:**
    - (A) Phagocytosis and lysosomal degradation are primary functions of macrophages, but the question specifically asks about the contribution related to the *upregulation of CD80/CD86*, which points towards antigen presentation and T cell activation, not just phagocytosis itself.
    - (B) Presentation of PAMPs is done by PRRs, not the primary function highlighted by CD80/CD86 upregulation. CD80/CD86 are involved in presenting processed *antigens* (derived from the pathogen) via MHC class II to T helper cells, initiating the adaptive response. This aligns perfectly with the macrophage's role as an APC after PRR activation.
    - (C) Secretion of perforin and granzymes is characteristic of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and Natural Killer (NK) cells, not macrophages in this context. Macrophages primarily kill via phagocytosis, reactive oxygen species, and nitric oxide.
    - (D) Release of histamine and inflammatory mediators is primarily associated with mast cells and basophils, although macrophages also release cytokines. However, CD80/CD86 upregulation is specifically linked to antigen presentation, not primarily histamine release.
    - (E) Production of antibodies is the function of B cells (plasma cells), not macrophages. While macrophages can present antigens to B cells (via CD40-CD40L interaction), they do not produce antibodies themselves.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Phagocytosis is a crucial function of macrophages, especially in clearing bacteria. However, the upregulation of CD80/CD86 specifically points to the macrophage's role as an antigen-presenting cell initiating the adaptive immune response by interacting with T cells. While phagocytosis precedes antigen presentation, option (B) more directly addresses the consequence of CD80/CD86 upregulation. Test-takers might choose this because phagocytosis is a fundamental macrophage function.
- (B): This is the correct answer. The upregulation of CD80 and CD86 on macrophages indicates their activation and maturation into potent APCs. Their primary role in this context is to present processed bacterial antigens via MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T helper cells, providing the necessary signals (MHC-peptide + CD80/86-CD28) for T cell activation and subsequent orchestration of the adaptive immune response.
- (C): Perforin and granzymes are cytotoxic molecules used by CTLs and NK cells to kill infected or cancerous cells. Macrophages do not typically function this way in response to bacterial infections; their primary killing mechanisms involve phagocytosis and intracellular degradation, or cytokine-mediated activation of other immune cells. This option confuses macrophage function with that of CTLs/NK cells.
- (D): Histamine release is primarily mediated by mast cells and basophils during allergic reactions or certain types of inflammation. While macrophages release various cytokines and chemokines that contribute to inflammation, CD80/CD86 upregulation is specifically related to antigen presentation and T cell activation, not histamine release. This option confuses macrophage function with that of mast cells/basophils.
- (E): Antibody production is the hallmark function of B lymphocytes (plasma cells). Macrophages can present antigens to B cells, but they do not produce antibodies themselves. This option confuses macrophage function with that of B cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Macrophage (Activated, CD80/86+) | CTL/NK Cell (Perforin/Granzymes) | Mast Cell/Basophil (Histamine) | B Cell (Antibodies) |
|----------------------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------|---------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Primary Function** | Antigen Presentation (MHC II)     | Cytotoxicity (Apoptosis)         | Inflammation (Vascular Permeability) | Antibody Production |
| **Key Molecules**    | MHC II, CD80, CD86                | Perforin, Granzymes              | Histamine, Leukotrienes          | Antibodies (IgG, IgM, etc.) |
| **Target**           | T Helper Cells (CD4+)             | Infected/Tumor Cells             | Endothelial Cells, Smooth Muscle | Pathogens, Antigens |
| **Relevance to Q**   | **Correct: Explains CD80/86 role** | Incorrect: Wrong cell type/mechanism | Incorrect: Wrong cell type/mechanism | Incorrect: Wrong cell type/mechanism |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Activated macrophages play a crucial role as antigen-presenting cells (APCs) by processing and presenting pathogen-derived antigens via MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T helper cells. Upregulation of costimulatory molecules like CD80 and CD86 is essential for effective T cell activation and the initiation of the adaptive immune response. Differentiating macrophage functions (phagocytosis, antigen presentation, cytokine production) from those of other immune cells (CTLs, NK cells, mast cells, B cells) is key.
